What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify creative adjectives through audience, references, observable choices, and delivery requirements.
  • Explain a scheduling dependency, quantify the immediate gap, and separate a revised estimate from spending approval.
  • Compare intended music use with documented permission and explain a publication hold precisely.
  • Coordinate version-specific talent approval and explain a schedule conflict without assuming silence means consent.
  • Compare distribution offers across fee, rights, territory, term, and exclusivity without inventing net value.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Creative Briefs and Development NotesWhat does premium and energetic actually mean?+

Your goal

Clarify creative adjectives through audience, references, observable choices, and delivery requirements.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

What does premium and energetic actually mean?

Client Sora asks director Ellis for a video that feels premium and energetic. The request contains no target audience, reference examples, duration, aspect ratio, or release channel. In the meeting, Sora confirms adult first-time buyers, a 30-second main film for the brand website, and a 16:9 frame. She wants deliberate product close-ups and energetic editing, not flashing transitions. A shorter, 15-second vertical social cutdown is only a possible later request. Ellis must document the agreed direction without treating that possible extra version as included or fully priced.

Sora: Client brand lead / Ellis: Creative director

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Who is the confirmed audience?

Answer and explanation

A. Adult first-time buyers

Sora explicitly identifies adult first-time buyers as the target audience in the meeting.

2. Which deliverable is confirmed?

Answer and explanation

B. A 30-second 16:9 film for the brand website

The confirmed duration, frame, and channel belong to the main website film only.

3. What remains only a possible later request?

Answer and explanation

D. A vertical social cutdown

The vertical version is not yet included, approved, or fully priced.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Energetic can refer to pacing, movement, music, or transitions. Here it means energetic editing, not flashing transitions. A reference is useful only when the team states which feature is being borrowed as inspiration.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
creative brief
A document setting the communication task and production requirements.clarify the creative brief
target audience
The intended group of viewers.define the target audience
single-minded proposition
The main message the communication should convey.sharpen the single-minded proposition
creative territory
A distinct direction for developing an idea.explore a creative territory
treatment
A written or visual account of the proposed creative approach.develop a treatment
mood board
A collection of visual references expressing a direction.review a mood board
reference film
An existing film used to explain a specific creative quality.select a reference film
storyboard
A sequence of images planning the shots or story.approve the storyboard
animatic
A timed sequence of storyboard images, often with sound.review an animatic
shot list
A list of planned shots for production.prepare the shot list
close-up
A shot framing a subject or detail tightly.capture a product close-up
pacing
The perceived rhythm and speed of an edit or sequence.adjust the pacing
transition
The change from one shot or scene to another.simplify the transition
visual hierarchy
The ordering of visual elements by importance.establish visual hierarchy
tone of voice
The characteristic style of the spoken or written message.define the tone of voice
call to action
The action the audience is asked to take.clarify the call to action
deliverable
A specified item or version to be supplied.confirm the deliverables
aspect ratio
The proportional relationship between frame width and height.specify the aspect ratio
running time
The duration of a film or version.confirm the running time
cutdown
A shorter version adapted from a longer piece.scope a cutdown
safe area
A defined frame area protecting essential content from cropping or overlays.check the safe area
supers
Text placed over the moving image.review the supers
brand lockup
An approved arrangement of brand marks and related elements.use the approved brand lockup
creative sign-off
Approval of a specified creative version or direction.record creative sign-off

A 16:9 main film does not automatically include a vertical adaptation. Reframing can affect shots, text, and approvals. Record a possible cutdown as a separate scope question until its requirements and authorization are established.

C Notice the language

Clarification is part of creative work

A direct request for examples can sound like resistance if it is detached from the client's aim. Acknowledge the desired impression, then offer a concrete distinction. Confirm the chosen interpretation so that later feedback has a shared reference point.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the direction
I understand that you want it to feel premium and energetic.
Request a concrete distinction
Do you mean faster pacing, more camera movement, or both?
Identify the viewer
Who needs to understand the product after watching?
Confirm the audience
We are addressing adult first-time buyers.
Pin down the duration
Is thirty seconds the confirmed main running time?
Name the delivery channel
The main film is for the brand website.
Specify the frame
The confirmed aspect ratio is sixteen by nine.
Limit the reference
Let us use the close-ups as a reference, not copy the entire film.
Translate the adjective
We will use deliberate product close-ups and an energetic edit.
Record the exclusion
Flashing transitions are not part of this direction.
Separate possible scope
The vertical cutdown remains a possible later request.
Ask about the message
What is the one product point the viewer must retain?
Check the action
What should the viewer do after the final frame?
Confirm the review stage
The treatment will come back for creative sign-off.
Avoid an unpriced commitment
I have not included a vertical version in this scope.
Close with a read-back
I will circulate the audience, style, format, and outstanding questions.
6 language and precision notes
Do you mean
Offers interpretations without pretending the adjective is already precise.
Confirmed
Distinguishes the current agreement from an idea still under discussion.
As a reference
Uses an example to explain a quality, not authorize copying.
Not part of
Makes an exclusion explicit and easy to verify.
Remains a possible
Prevents a tentative request from becoming an agreed deliverable.
The one ... point
Narrows the message so the team can prioritize the edit.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question best clarifies energetic?

Answer and explanation

C. Do you mean faster pacing, camera movement, or flashing transitions?

The correct question offers observable creative dimensions relevant to the ambiguous adjective.

2. Which read-back preserves the agreement?

Answer and explanation

B. Thirty seconds, 16:9, website, adult first-time buyers; vertical cutdown unconfirmed.

The accurate read-back retains the confirmed requirements and leaves the possible extra version unresolved.

3. What is a useful reference-film instruction?

Answer and explanation

C. Identify the particular pacing or framing feature to discuss.

A named feature makes the reference actionable without replacing the brief or authorizing copying.

4. Which statement overcommits scope?

Answer and explanation

C. The vertical adaptation is included even though it is only a possible request.

The possible later cutdown has not been included or fully priced in the stated scope.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

What does premium and energetic actually mean?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sora: Client brand lead / Ellis: Creative director

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • cutdown
  • close-up
  • target audience
  • reference film
  • call to action
  • pacing
  • running time
  • creative sign-off
  • creative brief
  • treatment
  1. Sora
    The last film looked polished, but it took too long to get going. Can this one feel premium without losing that first burst of energy?
  2. Ellis
    That helps. Let us sharpen the 1 before we choose a style. Who should the film speak to, and what should they understand about the product by the end?
  3. Sora
    Adult first-time buyers. They will see it on our brand website, and they should quickly understand what the product is for.
  4. Ellis
    Then the 2 needs a clear introduction. We should not rely on the product knowledge that an existing customer might already bring.
  5. Sora
    I like deliberate close shots of the product. I do not want the film to look cheap just because the edit is energetic.
  6. Ellis
    We can use a 3 to show important details and let the edit supply momentum. Faster does not have to mean flashing transitions or constant camera movement.
  7. Sora
    Exactly. No flashing transitions. The rhythm should be lively, but the product should remain easy to inspect.
  8. Ellis
    That makes 4 the main tool for energy. Do you have a reference where the rhythm feels right, even if the subject is different?
  9. Sora
    I can send one. I want the team to discuss the editing, not reproduce its entire visual style.
  10. Ellis
    Good. We will label it as a 5 for rhythm only. That avoids turning one useful example into an instruction to imitate every shot, color, or music choice.
  11. Sora
    The main film needs to be thirty seconds. It will appear in the normal wide player on the website.
  12. Ellis
    I will record a thirty-second 6 and a sixteen-by-nine frame. Those requirements help us judge how much explanation and product detail the edit can carry.
  13. Sora
    We may want a shorter, fifteen-second vertical version for social later. Please do not include it as confirmed scope yet.
  14. Ellis
    Then the 7 stays outside the confirmed scope for now. Vertical reframing may affect the shots and text, so I will not describe it as automatically included.
  15. Sora
    Please show us the approach before you organize a shoot.
  16. Ellis
    I will prepare a 8 with the audience, visual direction, pacing references, and main format. It will also show the questions we still need to settle.
  17. Sora
    We should agree what viewers are meant to do after watching. The final message needs some work.
  18. Ellis
    Yes, the 9 is still open. I will flag it instead of filling the end frame with a message that no one has approved.
  19. Sora
    So the confirmed direction is deliberate product detail, an energetic edit, no flashing transitions, and a thirty-second website film.
  20. Ellis
    Correct. We will seek 10 on that treatment. The optional vertical version and final action message will remain clearly separate until they are decided.

Answers and explanations
  1. creative brief Turn 2. Creative brief turns the desired impression into a shared communication task with specific production requirements.
  2. target audience Turn 4. Target audience identifies the intended adult first-time buyers, guiding explanation and creative choices rather than assuming existing product knowledge.
  3. close-up Turn 6. Close-up is a tight shot of the product or a detail, matching the client's specific visual preference.
  4. pacing Turn 8. Pacing describes the perceived rhythm and speed; energetic editing can supply it without the excluded flashing transitions.
  5. reference film Turn 10. Reference film provides an example of a named quality; it does not authorize copying or adopt every creative element.
  6. running time Turn 12. Running time specifies the duration of the main film, which the client confirms as thirty seconds.
  7. cutdown Turn 14. Cutdown is a shorter adapted version; the possible vertical version remains a separate scope question rather than an included deliverable.
  8. treatment Turn 16. Treatment presents the proposed creative approach for review before the production team commits to executing it.
  9. call to action Turn 18. Call to action identifies the requested viewer response; it remains a message decision rather than an assumed element.
  10. creative sign-off Turn 20. Creative sign-off approves a specified direction or version; this review should preserve the agreed requirements and outstanding decisions.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sora: The last film looked polished, but it took too long to get going. Can this one feel premium without losing that first burst of energy?
  2. Ellis: That helps. Let us sharpen the creative brief before we choose a style. Who should the film speak to, and what should they understand about the product by the end?
  3. Sora: Adult first-time buyers. They will see it on our brand website, and they should quickly understand what the product is for.
  4. Ellis: Then the target audience needs a clear introduction. We should not rely on the product knowledge that an existing customer might already bring.
  5. Sora: I like deliberate close shots of the product. I do not want the film to look cheap just because the edit is energetic.
  6. Ellis: We can use a close-up to show important details and let the edit supply momentum. Faster does not have to mean flashing transitions or constant camera movement.
  7. Sora: Exactly. No flashing transitions. The rhythm should be lively, but the product should remain easy to inspect.
  8. Ellis: That makes pacing the main tool for energy. Do you have a reference where the rhythm feels right, even if the subject is different?
  9. Sora: I can send one. I want the team to discuss the editing, not reproduce its entire visual style.
  10. Ellis: Good. We will label it as a reference film for rhythm only. That avoids turning one useful example into an instruction to imitate every shot, color, or music choice.
  11. Sora: The main film needs to be thirty seconds. It will appear in the normal wide player on the website.
  12. Ellis: I will record a thirty-second running time and a sixteen-by-nine frame. Those requirements help us judge how much explanation and product detail the edit can carry.
  13. Sora: We may want a shorter, fifteen-second vertical version for social later. Please do not include it as confirmed scope yet.
  14. Ellis: Then the cutdown stays outside the confirmed scope for now. Vertical reframing may affect the shots and text, so I will not describe it as automatically included.
  15. Sora: Please show us the approach before you organize a shoot.
  16. Ellis: I will prepare a treatment with the audience, visual direction, pacing references, and main format. It will also show the questions we still need to settle.
  17. Sora: We should agree what viewers are meant to do after watching. The final message needs some work.
  18. Ellis: Yes, the call to action is still open. I will flag it instead of filling the end frame with a message that no one has approved.
  19. Sora: So the confirmed direction is deliberate product detail, an energetic edit, no flashing transitions, and a thirty-second website film.
  20. Ellis: Correct. We will seek creative sign-off on that treatment. The optional vertical version and final action message will remain clearly separate until they are decided.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

What does premium sound like?

The producer clarifies the lesson's incomplete video brief with the client creative lead; audience and delivery requirements remain unknown.

  1. Producer: Your creative brief says premium and energetic. Which qualities should viewers actually notice in the video?
  2. Client creative lead: I haven't selected examples yet. I realize those words leave considerable room for interpretation.
  3. Producer: Would you like to discuss the audience first, before we propose music or an editing rhythm?
  4. Client creative lead: Yes, although I still need our marketing team to confirm who the primary audience is.
  5. Producer: Then I will mark the audience as unresolved. Who can clarify the intended deliverable and channels?
  6. Client creative lead: Our campaign manager can answer that. Are those details necessary before you explore the tone?
  7. Producer: We can explore possibilities, but we cannot assess brand fit confidently without that context.
  8. Client creative lead: Please show alternatives as discussion material, not as an agreed creative direction.
  9. Producer: Understood. Could you arrange a briefing with the campaign manager and bring two reference examples?
  10. Client creative lead: I'll request that meeting and confirm availability. The creative direction remains open until we clarify those points.
A trailer or a complete story?

Separate fictional case: a museum commissions a thirty-second exhibit trailer for its lobby screen, without specifying whether it should include narration.

  1. Creative director: The brief calls this a documentary, but the deliverable is a thirty-second lobby trailer.
  2. Museum communications lead: We want visitors to understand the exhibit. Is documentary the wrong word for that?
  3. Creative director: It suggests a fuller story. Should this piece explain the exhibit or encourage people to enter?
  4. Museum communications lead: Encourage them to enter. We can leave the detailed explanation to the exhibit itself.
  5. Creative director: That helps define the tone. Should we develop a visual invitation rather than a narrated summary?
  6. Museum communications lead: Possibly, but I haven't checked whether sound is available on that screen.
  7. Creative director: Could you confirm that before we build a concept around a voice-over?
  8. Museum communications lead: Yes. Meanwhile, can you explore a version that communicates through images and short captions?
  9. Creative director: Certainly. I'll label sound as unconfirmed and focus the creative brief on attracting visitors.
  10. Museum communications lead: Good. I'll check the screen setup, then review the concept for accuracy and brand fit.
Two meanings of playful

Separate fictional case: a podcast producer and a commissioning editor discuss a gardening series for first-time apartment gardeners; the brief requests a playful tone.

  1. Podcast producer: When you ask for a playful tone, do you mean comedy or an approachable conversation?
  2. Commissioning editor: An approachable conversation. The listeners are first-time apartment gardeners, and we shouldn't make mistakes embarrassing.
  3. Podcast producer: So practical curiosity would fit better than jokes about people who cannot keep plants alive?
  4. Commissioning editor: Exactly. The brand fit depends on welcoming beginners, not presenting the host as superior.
  5. Podcast producer: Could we use a host who asks the questions a new gardener might hesitate to ask?
  6. Commissioning editor: Yes, provided the expert answers respectfully. What would you bring to the development review?
  7. Podcast producer: A sample opening and an episode outline, unless you expect a fully recorded pilot.
  8. Commissioning editor: An outline is sufficient. Please don't book talent or studio time for this review.
  9. Podcast producer: Then the deliverable is an outline with a sample opening, and no production booking.
  10. Commissioning editor: Correct. That gives us enough to test the tone before deciding whether to commission a pilot.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What does premium and energetic actually mean?
  1. Read turns 1-10; turn the two style adjectives into observable production choices.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the main film from the shorter optional version.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Read back a second creative brief

A client confirms a 45-second film for returning subscribers, in a 1:1 frame, for an email-linked campaign page. A six-second teaser is only a possible later request.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Director: "The confirmed running time is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 45 seconds

Forty-five seconds is the duration explicitly agreed for the main film.

2. Client: "The target audience is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. returning subscribers

The briefing names returning subscribers rather than first-time buyers as the intended viewers.

3. Director: "The confirmed aspect ratio is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 1:1

One to one is the square frame specified for this main version.

4. Client: "The unconfirmed later version is a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. six-second teaser

The teaser remains a possible later request and must not be treated as included scope.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Creative Briefs and Development Notes
Original fictional case: Client Sora asks director Ellis for a video that feels premium and energetic. The request contains no target audience, reference examples, duration, aspect ratio, or release channel. In the meeting, Sora confirms adult first-time buyers, a 30-second main film for the brand website, and a 16:9 frame. She wants deliberate product close-ups and energetic editing, not flashing transitions. A shorter, 15-second vertical social cutdown is only a possible later request. Ellis must document the agreed direction without treating that possible extra version as included or fully priced.
Language workshop: What does premium and energetic actually mean?
Communication goal: Clarify creative adjectives through audience, references, observable choices, and delivery requirements.
Language guidance: Energetic can refer to pacing, movement, music, or transitions. Here it means energetic editing, not flashing transitions. A reference is useful only when the team states which feature is being borrowed as inspiration.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that you want it to feel premium and energetic. / Do you mean faster pacing, more camera movement, or both? / Who needs to understand the product after watching? / We are addressing adult first-time buyers. / Is thirty seconds the confirmed main running time? / The main film is for the brand website. / The confirmed aspect ratio is sixteen by nine. / Let us use the close-ups as a reference, not copy the entire film. / We will use deliberate product close-ups and an energetic edit. / Flashing transitions are not part of this direction. / The vertical cutdown remains a possible later request. / What is the one product point the viewer must retain? / What should the viewer do after the final frame? / The treatment will come back for creative sign-off. / I have not included a vertical version in this scope. / I will circulate the audience, style, format, and outstanding questions.
Vocabulary: creative brief: A document setting the communication task and production requirements. / target audience: The intended group of viewers. / single-minded proposition: The main message the communication should convey. / creative territory: A distinct direction for developing an idea. / treatment: A written or visual account of the proposed creative approach. / mood board: A collection of visual references expressing a direction. / reference film: An existing film used to explain a specific creative quality. / storyboard: A sequence of images planning the shots or story. / animatic: A timed sequence of storyboard images, often with sound. / shot list: A list of planned shots for production. / close-up: A shot framing a subject or detail tightly. / pacing: The perceived rhythm and speed of an edit or sequence. / transition: The change from one shot or scene to another. / visual hierarchy: The ordering of visual elements by importance. / tone of voice: The characteristic style of the spoken or written message. / call to action: The action the audience is asked to take. / deliverable: A specified item or version to be supplied. / aspect ratio: The proportional relationship between frame width and height. / running time: The duration of a film or version. / cutdown: A shorter version adapted from a longer piece. / safe area: A defined frame area protecting essential content from cropping or overlays. / supers: Text placed over the moving image. / brand lockup: An approved arrangement of brand marks and related elements. / creative sign-off: Approval of a specified creative version or direction.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; turn the two style adjectives into observable production choices. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the main film from the shorter optional version. Complete the transfer and read back audience, duration, frame, and unconfirmed scope.
Partner: Sora: Client brand lead / Ellis: Creative director
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A client confirms a 45-second film for returning subscribers, in a 1:1 frame, for an email-linked campaign page. A six-second teaser is only a possible later request.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sora: We should agree what viewers are meant to do after watching. The final message needs some work.
Ellis: Yes, the call to action is still open. I will flag it instead of filling the end frame with a message that no one has approved.
Sora: So the confirmed direction is deliberate product detail, an energetic edit, no flashing transitions, and a thirty-second website film.
Ellis: Correct. We will seek creative sign-off on that treatment. The optional vertical version and final action message will remain clearly separate until they are decided.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Creative Briefs and Development Notes
Original fictional case: Client Sora asks director Ellis for a video that feels premium and energetic. The request contains no target audience, reference examples, duration, aspect ratio, or release channel. In the meeting, Sora confirms adult first-time buyers, a 30-second main film for the brand website, and a 16:9 frame. She wants deliberate product close-ups and energetic editing, not flashing transitions. A shorter, 15-second vertical social cutdown is only a possible later request. Ellis must document the agreed direction without treating that possible extra version as included or fully priced.
Language workshop: What does premium and energetic actually mean?
Communication goal: Clarify creative adjectives through audience, references, observable choices, and delivery requirements.
Language guidance: Energetic can refer to pacing, movement, music, or transitions. Here it means energetic editing, not flashing transitions. A reference is useful only when the team states which feature is being borrowed as inspiration.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that you want it to feel premium and energetic. / Do you mean faster pacing, more camera movement, or both? / Who needs to understand the product after watching? / We are addressing adult first-time buyers. / Is thirty seconds the confirmed main running time? / The main film is for the brand website. / The confirmed aspect ratio is sixteen by nine. / Let us use the close-ups as a reference, not copy the entire film. / We will use deliberate product close-ups and an energetic edit. / Flashing transitions are not part of this direction. / The vertical cutdown remains a possible later request. / What is the one product point the viewer must retain? / What should the viewer do after the final frame? / The treatment will come back for creative sign-off. / I have not included a vertical version in this scope. / I will circulate the audience, style, format, and outstanding questions.
Vocabulary: creative brief: A document setting the communication task and production requirements. / target audience: The intended group of viewers. / single-minded proposition: The main message the communication should convey. / creative territory: A distinct direction for developing an idea. / treatment: A written or visual account of the proposed creative approach. / mood board: A collection of visual references expressing a direction. / reference film: An existing film used to explain a specific creative quality. / storyboard: A sequence of images planning the shots or story. / animatic: A timed sequence of storyboard images, often with sound. / shot list: A list of planned shots for production. / close-up: A shot framing a subject or detail tightly. / pacing: The perceived rhythm and speed of an edit or sequence. / transition: The change from one shot or scene to another. / visual hierarchy: The ordering of visual elements by importance. / tone of voice: The characteristic style of the spoken or written message. / call to action: The action the audience is asked to take. / deliverable: A specified item or version to be supplied. / aspect ratio: The proportional relationship between frame width and height. / running time: The duration of a film or version. / cutdown: A shorter version adapted from a longer piece. / safe area: A defined frame area protecting essential content from cropping or overlays. / supers: Text placed over the moving image. / brand lockup: An approved arrangement of brand marks and related elements. / creative sign-off: Approval of a specified creative version or direction.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; turn the two style adjectives into observable production choices. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the main film from the shorter optional version. Complete the transfer and read back audience, duration, frame, and unconfirmed scope.
Partner: Sora: Client brand lead / Ellis: Creative director
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A client confirms a 45-second film for returning subscribers, in a 1:1 frame, for an email-linked campaign page. A six-second teaser is only a possible later request.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sora: We should agree what viewers are meant to do after watching. The final message needs some work.
Ellis: Yes, the call to action is still open. I will flag it instead of filling the end frame with a message that no one has approved.
Sora: So the confirmed direction is deliberate product detail, an energetic edit, no flashing transitions, and a thirty-second website film.
Ellis: Correct. We will seek creative sign-off on that treatment. The optional vertical version and final action message will remain clearly separate until they are decided.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Production Planning and BudgetThe location move does not fit the call sheet+

Your goal

Explain a scheduling dependency, quantify the immediate gap, and separate a revised estimate from spending approval.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The location move does not fit the call sheet

A revised shoot plan ends work at Location A at 12:00 and starts filming at Location B at 12:30. The company move takes 35 minutes, followed by 45 minutes of setup; these tasks cannot overlap in this plan. The earliest filming start is therefore 13:20, before any extra delay. The approved budget is $25,000. New transport costs $750 and added equipment costs $250; possible overtime remains unpriced. Producer Leena and first assistant director Grant must revise the plan without cutting required breaks or treating the new costs as approved.

Leena: Producer / Grant: First assistant director

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the earliest filming start at B on the stated assumptions?

Answer and explanation

A. 13:20

Thirty-five minutes of movement plus forty-five minutes of setup after noon gives thirteen twenty.

2. How much known additional cost has been identified?

Answer and explanation

B. $1,000

Transport of $750 plus equipment of $250 totals $1,000 in identified additions.

3. Which cost remains unpriced?

Answer and explanation

D. Possible overtime

The briefing explicitly leaves possible overtime unpriced rather than assuming it costs nothing.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The 35-minute move and 45-minute setup are consecutive in this plan: 80 minutes after 12:00 gives 13:20. The 12:30 filming slot is short by 50 minutes, even without additional delay.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
call sheet
The daily production document listing timing, locations, and key information.revise the call sheet
company move
Relocation of the production unit between locations.schedule the company move
setup
Preparation of a location, equipment, and scene for filming.allow setup time
first assistant director (first AD)
The role coordinating on-set scheduling and execution.consult the first AD
line producer
The role managing practical production resources and costs.brief the line producer
shooting schedule
The planned sequence and timing of filming work.update the shooting schedule
stripboard
A visual scheduling arrangement of scenes or production work.revise the stripboard
call time
The time a person or department is required to report.confirm the call time
wrap time
The time filming or a person's scheduled work ends.estimate the wrap time
turnaround
The interval between one work period's end and the next call.protect required turnaround
meal break
A scheduled break for eating under applicable conditions.preserve the meal break
overtime
Work beyond the relevant agreed or regulated hours.estimate overtime exposure
contingency
A planned reserve for specified uncertainties.review the contingency
cost report
A record of production spending, commitments, and forecasts.update the cost report
committed cost
An amount already obligated under a relevant agreement.record committed costs
actual cost
An amount incurred and recorded on the stated basis.reconcile actual costs
forecast cost
An estimate of expected cost at a defined point or completion.revise forecast costs
change order
A document recording an authorized change to agreed work.obtain a change order
location permit
Required permission to use a location under applicable rules.verify the location permit
location release
An agreement concerning filming or use of a location.check the location release
technical scout
A site visit assessing practical production requirements.arrange a technical scout
load-in
Bringing equipment into a production location.coordinate load-in
strike
Dismantling and removing a set or equipment.schedule the strike
critical dependency
A required condition or preceding task affecting the plan.identify the critical dependency

The known additions bring the cost estimate to at least $26,000 before unpriced overtime and any other verified changes. This is not a new approved budget. Do not use an estimate to imply permission to spend.

C Notice the language

A precise no can preserve the shoot

Production teams may feel pressure to accept an optimistic schedule to keep a client happy. State the dependency and arithmetic, then offer a planning decision. Avoid making the crew absorb an impossible promise through skipped safeguards or undocumented overtime.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the conflict
The twelve-thirty filming slot does not fit the location move.
Show the dependency
Setup begins only after the thirty-five-minute move.
Do the arithmetic
Thirty-five plus forty-five gives eighty minutes.
State the earliest start
The earliest filming start is thirteen twenty on these assumptions.
Quantify the shortfall
The current slot is fifty minutes too early.
Preserve the qualifications
That excludes any additional delay.
Separate the known costs
Transport adds $750 and equipment adds $250.
State the estimate
Known costs bring the estimate to at least $26,000.
Keep uncertainty visible
Possible overtime has not yet been priced.
Protect authorization
The approved budget remains $25,000 until the change is authorized.
Request a decision
We need an approved schedule and cost revision.
Avoid unsafe compression
Required breaks and safeguards cannot become our hidden buffer.
Offer a planning route
We can review the scene order with the first AD.
Check the location terms
Confirm access and permit conditions before changing the timing.
Update connected records
The call sheet and cost report must reflect the same decision.
Close with ownership
I will circulate the revised option for approval, not label it approved.
6 language and precision notes
Only after
Shows a task dependency, not just a preferred order.
Earliest
Gives a lower timing bound under stated assumptions.
At least
Marks a cost floor while specified costs remain unpriced.
Until ... authorized
Preserves the current approval boundary.
Hidden buffer
Names unplanned time taken from other obligations or safeguards.
For approval
Identifies a proposed revision rather than a completed authorization.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which timing statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Filming can start no earlier than 13:20 under the stated plan.

Consecutive movement and setup require eighty minutes after noon, making thirteen twenty the earliest stated start.

2. Which cost statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. Known additions total $1,000; overtime remains unpriced.

The two priced additions total one thousand dollars, while possible overtime is explicitly unresolved.

3. Which wording distinguishes estimate and authority?

Answer and explanation

B. The revised estimate needs approval before it becomes an authorized budget change.

Estimating the cost does not supply the separate authorization required for changing the budget.

4. Which response preserves the stated safeguards?

Answer and explanation

D. Revise scene order and timing while preserving required breaks and conditions.

A schedule revision must address the real dependency without relying on skipped safeguards or invented approvals.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

The location move does not fit the call sheet

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leena: Producer / Grant: First assistant director

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • meal break
  • cost report
  • shooting schedule
  • forecast cost
  • location permit
  • setup
  • call sheet

  • change order
  • company move
  1. Leena
    Can you sanity-check the second-location plan? We wrap at A at noon, but the client has put the first interview at B at twelve thirty.
  2. Grant
    That 1 does not fit the tasks. The move takes thirty-five minutes, and setup at B takes another forty-five. Those steps cannot overlap in this plan.
  3. Leena
    So the thirty-minute gap is not enough even for the move alone.
  4. Grant
    Correct. The 2 finishes at twelve thirty-five at the earliest. Then we still need setup. We should show the full dependency instead of compressing it into a travel note.
  5. Leena
    That makes the earliest filming start thirteen twenty, before any extra delay.
  6. Grant
    Yes. The 3 cannot disappear because the client prefers the earlier slot. We need to revise the scene order or timing with the people responsible for the work.
  7. Leena
    I will ask about moving the interview. Meanwhile, should we flag the timing conflict before anyone distributes that call sheet?
  8. Grant
    Revise the 4 once the plan is agreed, and flag the current conflict clearly in the meantime. The crew should not receive an impossible time as though it were settled.
  9. Leena
    The transport quote adds seven hundred fifty dollars, and the extra equipment adds two hundred fifty.
  10. Grant
    Record those in the 5. They total one thousand dollars. The timing change may also affect overtime, but we have not priced that exposure yet.
  11. Leena
    The current approved budget is twenty-five thousand. Can I say the revised budget is twenty-six thousand?
  12. Grant
    Call that a 6 floor, not an approved budget. Known additions bring us to at least twenty-six thousand, and the possible overtime remains an open amount.
  13. Leena
    We also need to know whether Location B permits the later filming and exit times.
  14. Grant
    Check the 7 and the relevant access agreement. Moving the interview may solve one conflict while creating another, so we should confirm the actual conditions.
  15. Leena
    Someone suggested using the crew's break to recover the lost time. I do not want that assumed in the plan.
  16. Grant
    Agreed. The 8 and other required safeguards stay protected. We must solve the schedule within the relevant working conditions, not hide the gap in time the crew is entitled to.
  17. Leena
    I will present a revised timing option with the known costs and the overtime question.
  18. Grant
    Route the proposed 9 through the approval process. A client request explains why the change is being assessed, but it does not make every consequence approved.
  19. Leena
    Once a decision is made, both the crew schedule and the financial record should reflect it.
  20. Grant
    Exactly. Keep the 10 visible: move first, setup next, filming after that. Then the team can make a real production decision instead of inheriting an optimistic thirty-minute gap.

Answers and explanations
  1. shooting schedule Turn 2. Shooting schedule sets the planned filming sequence and times; the proposed slot conflicts with the stated move and setup.
  2. company move Turn 4. Company move is the thirty-five-minute relocation between sites, preceding the separate forty-five-minute setup in this plan.
  3. setup Turn 6. Setup requires forty-five additional minutes after arrival, producing the thirteen-twenty earliest filming time.
  4. call sheet Turn 8. Call sheet communicates the operational day's timing and locations; it must reflect the authorized workable plan.
  5. cost report Turn 10. Cost report tracks spending, commitments, and forecasts; the two known additions need visibility without implying approval.
  6. forecast cost Turn 12. Forecast cost is an estimate; twenty-six thousand includes the known additions but excludes still-unpriced overtime.
  7. location permit Turn 14. Location permit contains applicable permission conditions; a scheduling proposal does not establish permission for changed hours.
  8. meal break Turn 16. Meal break is a required or agreed rest period under the applicable conditions, not an unapproved scheduling buffer.
  9. change order Turn 18. Change order records an authorized alteration to agreed work; the proposed timing and cost revision still needs that decision.
  10. critical dependency Turn 20. Critical dependency identifies the required move-then-setup sequence that must remain visible in any revised production plan.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The move is not yet scheduled

A production manager updates a producer on the lesson's added location change; transport time and the production budget are still unrevised.

  1. Producer: The schedule now includes the second location. Can I tell the client the move is fully planned?
  2. Production manager: The location change is listed, but transport time and the production budget have not been revised.
  3. Producer: So the shooting blocks are visible, but the travel between them is still missing?
  4. Production manager: Correct. The current schedule should not be treated as a workable final call sheet.
  5. Producer: What needs to happen before we can give the crew reliable arrival times?
  6. Production manager: We need a transport estimate and a review of how the move affects the shooting day.
  7. Producer: Does adding the location also mean that any required access or permit is confirmed?
  8. Production manager: No. Listing a location does not establish its access status; that needs a separate check.
  9. Producer: I'll describe the plan as provisional and avoid promising that the original budget still holds.
  10. Production manager: I'll coordinate the outstanding checks and return with their scheduling and cost implications.
Rain cover remains an option

Separate fictional case: a commercial shoot has a confirmed outdoor booking; an indoor rain-cover quote is available but has not been accepted.

  1. Line producer: The outdoor booking is confirmed. The indoor rain-cover location is only quoted, not reserved.
  2. Agency producer: The client saw both addresses on the schedule and thought we had secured both spaces.
  3. Line producer: I should distinguish their status more clearly. The indoor option still needs a booking decision.
  4. Agency producer: Can you put the indoor address on the call sheet now, just in case?
  5. Line producer: Not as a confirmed reporting location. That could send crew to a space we haven't booked.
  6. Agency producer: What can we tell the client about the production budget without overstating progress?
  7. Line producer: The main location is booked; the rain-cover cost is quoted and awaits approval.
  8. Agency producer: Please also explain what is excluded from the confirmed arrangement, rather than saying everything is covered.
  9. Line producer: Agreed. I'll keep the contingency separate and ask the location manager about the option's availability.
  10. Agency producer: I'll take the cost question to the client and report the decision before we change crew instructions.
The revised call sheet has not reached everyone

Separate fictional case: a studio shoot's revised call sheet changes the makeup call to seven; camera crew receipts are confirmed, but makeup receipt is not.

  1. Production coordinator: The revised call sheet is out, and camera has confirmed receipt. Makeup's acknowledgment is still missing.
  2. Assistant director: Then everyone knows about the seven o'clock makeup call, apart from that final confirmation?
  3. Production coordinator: We know it was sent, not that makeup has seen it. Those are different states.
  4. Assistant director: You're right. The earlier sheet could still be the version they are using.
  5. Production coordinator: I'll contact the makeup lead directly and ask them to repeat the updated call time.
  6. Assistant director: Please check whether that earlier call changes their booked hours or the production budget.
  7. Production coordinator: I can ask, but I don't have confirmation of an additional charge at this point.
  8. Assistant director: Keep that cost as pending rather than assuming the revised schedule has no effect.
  9. Production coordinator: My update will separate distribution, acknowledgment, and any cost adjustment that needs approval.
  10. Assistant director: Good. Let me know when makeup confirms so I can settle the opening setup with the crew.

  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish known additions from unpriced overtime.

Calculate a second location move

Filming at Site C ends at 10:00. A 25-minute move is followed by 35 minutes of setup, with no overlap. New transport costs $400 and equipment $150; both need approval.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Producer: "The total move-and-setup time is ___ minutes."

Answer and explanation

B. 60

Twenty-five minutes of movement plus thirty-five minutes of setup totals sixty minutes.

2. First AD: "The earliest filming start is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 11:00

Sixty consecutive minutes after ten o'clock gives an earliest start of eleven.

3. Producer: "The known additional cost is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

A. 550

Four hundred dollars of transport plus one hundred fifty of equipment totals five hundred fifty.

4. First AD: "Those additional costs still need ___."

Answer and explanation

D. approval

The briefing expressly states that the priced additions have not yet been authorized.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Production Planning and Budget
Original fictional case: A revised shoot plan ends work at Location A at 12:00 and starts filming at Location B at 12:30. The company move takes 35 minutes, followed by 45 minutes of setup; these tasks cannot overlap in this plan. The earliest filming start is therefore 13:20, before any extra delay. The approved budget is $25,000. New transport costs $750 and added equipment costs $250; possible overtime remains unpriced. Producer Leena and first assistant director Grant must revise the plan without cutting required breaks or treating the new costs as approved.
Language workshop: The location move does not fit the call sheet
Communication goal: Explain a scheduling dependency, quantify the immediate gap, and separate a revised estimate from spending approval.
Language guidance: The 35-minute move and 45-minute setup are consecutive in this plan: 80 minutes after 12:00 gives 13:20. The 12:30 filming slot is short by 50 minutes, even without additional delay.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The twelve-thirty filming slot does not fit the location move. / Setup begins only after the thirty-five-minute move. / Thirty-five plus forty-five gives eighty minutes. / The earliest filming start is thirteen twenty on these assumptions. / The current slot is fifty minutes too early. / That excludes any additional delay. / Transport adds $750 and equipment adds $250. / Known costs bring the estimate to at least $26,000. / Possible overtime has not yet been priced. / The approved budget remains $25,000 until the change is authorized. / We need an approved schedule and cost revision. / Required breaks and safeguards cannot become our hidden buffer. / We can review the scene order with the first AD. / Confirm access and permit conditions before changing the timing. / The call sheet and cost report must reflect the same decision. / I will circulate the revised option for approval, not label it approved.
Vocabulary: call sheet: The daily production document listing timing, locations, and key information. / company move: Relocation of the production unit between locations. / setup: Preparation of a location, equipment, and scene for filming. / first assistant director (first AD): The role coordinating on-set scheduling and execution. / line producer: The role managing practical production resources and costs. / shooting schedule: The planned sequence and timing of filming work. / stripboard: A visual scheduling arrangement of scenes or production work. / call time: The time a person or department is required to report. / wrap time: The time filming or a person's scheduled work ends. / turnaround: The interval between one work period's end and the next call. / meal break: A scheduled break for eating under applicable conditions. / overtime: Work beyond the relevant agreed or regulated hours. / contingency: A planned reserve for specified uncertainties. / cost report: A record of production spending, commitments, and forecasts. / committed cost: An amount already obligated under a relevant agreement. / actual cost: An amount incurred and recorded on the stated basis. / forecast cost: An estimate of expected cost at a defined point or completion. / change order: A document recording an authorized change to agreed work. / location permit: Required permission to use a location under applicable rules. / location release: An agreement concerning filming or use of a location. / technical scout: A site visit assessing practical production requirements. / load-in: Bringing equipment into a production location. / strike: Dismantling and removing a set or equipment. / critical dependency: A required condition or preceding task affecting the plan.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; give the move, setup, and earliest filming times in sequence. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish known additions from unpriced overtime. Complete the transfer without overlapping the two consecutive tasks.
Partner: Leena: Producer / Grant: First assistant director
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Filming at Site C ends at 10:00. A 25-minute move is followed by 35 minutes of setup, with no overlap. New transport costs $400 and equipment $150; both need approval.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leena: I will present a revised timing option with the known costs and the overtime question.
Grant: Route the proposed change order through the approval process. A client request explains why the change is being assessed, but it does not make every consequence approved.
Leena: Once a decision is made, both the crew schedule and the financial record should reflect it.
Grant: Exactly. Keep the critical dependency visible: move first, setup next, filming after that. Then the team can make a real production decision instead of inheriting an optimistic thirty-minute gap.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Rights, Clearances, and LicensingA music receipt is not the release clearance+

Your goal

Compare intended music use with documented permission and explain a publication hold precisely.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A music receipt is not the release clearance

Editor Ada has placed track M17 in a campaign video. A receipt is in the folder, but the available license document describes only organic use on the brand website in the United States from 1 June to 31 August. The planned campaign also includes paid social in the United States and Canada through 30 November. Clearance coordinator Ben has not verified the necessary composition and recording permissions for that expanded use. Under this production's release process, the video stays on hold until the required scope is confirmed or an appropriately cleared replacement is used.

Ada: Editor / Ben: Clearance coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which use is described in the available document?

Answer and explanation

D. Organic brand-website use in the United States, 1 June to 31 August

The available document states a limited channel, territory, and term that do not match the whole planned campaign.

2. Which planned use extends beyond that description?

Answer and explanation

A. Paid social in Canada through 30 November

Paid social, Canada, and the later end date exceed the available document's stated scope.

3. What is the local publication status?

Answer and explanation

A. On hold pending confirmed rights or an appropriately cleared replacement

The release process requires confirmed scope or a cleared replacement rather than assumptions based on payment or duration.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The composition and the specific recording are distinct rights subjects. A single provider may administer both, but the team must verify its authority and the actual grant. Do not assume a receipt establishes every required permission.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
clearance
Confirmation of permissions required for a particular use.complete music clearance
license scope
The boundaries of a granted permission.verify the license scope
musical composition
The underlying music and any lyrics.identify the musical composition
sound recording
A particular recorded performance or production of sound.identify the sound recording
synchronization license
Permission concerning music used in timed relation to visuals.review the synchronization license
master-use permission
Permission to use a particular sound recording.confirm master-use permission
rights holder
The party controlling a relevant legal right.identify the rights holder
music publisher
An entity administering relevant rights in musical works.contact the music publisher
record label
A company involved in recorded music and potentially controlling recording rights.verify the record label's authority
chain of title
Records showing ownership or transfer of relevant rights.examine the chain of title
territory
The geographical area covered by permission.confirm the licensed territory
license term
The period during which the permitted use is authorized.check the license term
paid media
Placement or distribution supported by advertising spend.clear paid-media use
organic use
Distribution without paid amplification in the specified context.confirm organic-use rights
usage restriction
A condition limiting how material may be used.identify usage restrictions
cue sheet
A record of music used in a production and relevant details.prepare a cue sheet
temporary track
Music used provisionally while the final choice or clearance is pending.replace the temporary track
library music
Music offered through a catalog under applicable licensing terms.review library-music terms
royalty-free
A licensing description whose actual payment and use terms must be checked.verify royalty-free terms
public domain
A status in which relevant copyright protection does not apply in the jurisdiction concerned.assess public-domain status
release hold
A block on publication pending a required condition.maintain the release hold
clearance log
A record of rights requests, documents, decisions, and gaps.update the clearance log
rights extension
An agreed expansion of the period or scope of permission.request a rights extension
replacement cue
A substitute music selection for a production.assess a replacement cue

The planned channel, territory, and end date exceed the available description. Shortening the extract or calling it royalty-free does not establish the needed scope. A cue sheet records usage; it is not itself a substitute for permission.

C Notice the language

Separate creative preference from permission

A track can become emotionally attached to an edit, making a clearance objection feel like a creative rejection. Explain the missing permission dimensions and the available review routes. A temporary track is a production choice, not evidence of public-use rights.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the asset
We are reviewing music track M17.
Separate payment and permission
The receipt does not establish the complete license scope.
Read the actual document
The document describes organic website use in the United States.
State the documented term
The listed term runs from 1 June to 31 August.
Compare the plan
The campaign also includes paid social and Canada through November.
Name the gaps
Channel, territory, and term all need confirmation.
Distinguish rights subjects
We need the relevant composition and recording permissions checked.
Verify authority
Confirm that the licensor controls the rights it claims to grant.
Protect publication
The video remains on hold under our release process.
Avoid a duration shortcut
A shorter extract does not automatically solve the clearance issue.
Offer a viable route
We can seek the expanded rights or assess a cleared replacement.
Keep an alternative conditional
The replacement also needs its intended uses verified.
Record the evidence
Link the license and scope decision in the clearance log.
Distinguish a usage record
A cue sheet documents the music; it does not grant the rights.
Separate edit and release
The creative edit can be ready while public release remains blocked.
Close with a clear condition
Release follows confirmed scope, not an assumed meaning of royalty-free.
6 language and precision notes
Describes
Reports what the available document says without inventing a broader grant.
Also includes
Identifies the planned uses missing from the narrower document.
Need confirmation
Names an unresolved permission question, not proof of infringement.
Remains on hold
States the local release status until the specified condition changes.
Controls the rights
Checks the licensor's authority rather than relying on a familiar brand.
Can be ready while
Separates creative completion from legal or operational release readiness.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sentence best identifies the mismatch?

Answer and explanation

A. The plan extends the documented channel, territory, and term.

The planned paid social, Canadian territory, and November end date exceed the described grant.

2. What must be distinguished when checking this recorded track?

Answer and explanation

A. The underlying composition and the particular recording

The musical work and its recorded performance can involve distinct permissions and controlling parties.

3. Which item is not itself a rights grant?

Answer and explanation

D. A cue sheet listing the music used

A cue sheet records use and details, but listing the track does not itself grant permission.

4. Which next action respects the release process?

Answer and explanation

D. Seek the expanded permissions or verify a replacement before release.

The stated process requires actual clearance of the intended use rather than a labeling or duration shortcut.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A music receipt is not the release clearance

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ada: Editor / Ben: Clearance coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank

  • master-use permission
  • cue sheet
  • license scope

  • clearance log
