  2. Ben
    The payment record does not establish the full 1. The document we have describes organic brand-website use in the United States from June first through August thirty-first.
  3. Ada
    Our campaign includes paid social as well. Does that fall within the website permission?
  4. Ben
    We have not confirmed 2 coverage. We need the actual grant checked against that plan. I cannot turn organic website wording into paid social permission just because the same video is involved.
  5. Ada
    We also planned to run the campaign in Canada. The music file itself does not mention countries.
  6. Ben
    The relevant 3 is in the license, not inferred from the file. The document names the United States only, so Canada needs to be addressed in the rights review.
  7. Ada
    And our paid campaign continues through the end of November.
  8. Ben
    That also exceeds the documented 4. We have an August end date in the available document and a November end date in the plan. Those dates need an explicit resolution.
  9. Ada
    The provider said it was library music. Should we contact the composer or the performer?
  10. Ben
    First verify who controls the relevant 5 and recording rights. One provider may administer both, but we need evidence of authority and scope rather than guess from the track label.
  11. Ada
    So permission for the song does not automatically tell us whether this exact recording is covered.
  12. Ben
    Correct. Check the applicable 6 as well. We should map the intended audiovisual use to the permissions actually granted by the authorized parties.
  13. Ada
    The campaign team wants to keep the date. Would using a shorter extract change the clearance question, or would we still need the wider grant?
  14. Ben
    A shorter extract does not establish clearance. The 7 stays in place under our process until the necessary scope is confirmed or an appropriately cleared replacement is used.
  15. Ada
    I can identify another cue, but I assume we must check that one too.
  16. Ben
    Yes. A 8 is an alternative, not a shortcut around the review. Give the rights team the channels, countries, dates, and versions so it can assess the actual use.
  17. Ada
    I will keep the creative export separate from the publication approval and include the relevant documents.
  18. Ben
    Put the decision and remaining gaps in the 9. That helps the next reviewer see why the edit can be finished while release is still pending.
  19. Ada
    We also need to prepare the music usage record for delivery.
  20. Ben
    Prepare the 10, but keep its purpose clear. It records the cue used; it does not replace the rights grant or turn the unresolved campaign scope into an approval.

Answers and explanations
  1. license scope Turn 2. License scope defines permitted uses; a receipt alone does not show the required channel, territory, term, and rights coverage.
  2. paid media Turn 4. Paid media involves purchased advertising placement or amplification, which is additional to the organic website use described in the document.
  3. territory Turn 6. Territory specifies the geographic permission boundary; the document names the United States, while the plan also includes Canada.
  4. license term Turn 8. License term is the authorized period; the available August end date does not establish permission through November.
  5. musical composition Turn 10. Musical composition is the underlying work, distinct from the specific sound recording used in the edit.
  6. master-use permission Turn 12. Master-use permission concerns the particular recording, so composition permission alone cannot be assumed to cover that recording.
  7. release hold Turn 14. Release hold blocks publication under this production's process while the intended-use permissions remain unconfirmed.
  8. replacement cue Turn 16. Replacement cue is an alternative selection; it still needs permissions matching the actual planned use before release.
  9. clearance log Turn 18. Clearance log preserves documents, scope decisions, and unresolved questions so the release team can trace the actual permission basis.
  10. cue sheet Turn 20. Cue sheet records music use and details; it supports documentation but is not itself the permission to use the music.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ada: M17 finally makes the edit work. I found the receipt, but can you check clearance before I mark the export ready for release?
  2. Ben: The payment record does not establish the full license scope. The document we have describes organic brand-website use in the United States from June first through August thirty-first.
  3. Ada: Our campaign includes paid social as well. Does that fall within the website permission?
  4. Ben: We have not confirmed paid media coverage. We need the actual grant checked against that plan. I cannot turn organic website wording into paid social permission just because the same video is involved.
  5. Ada: We also planned to run the campaign in Canada. The music file itself does not mention countries.
  6. Ben: The relevant territory is in the license, not inferred from the file. The document names the United States only, so Canada needs to be addressed in the rights review.
  7. Ada: And our paid campaign continues through the end of November.
  8. Ben: That also exceeds the documented license term. We have an August end date in the available document and a November end date in the plan. Those dates need an explicit resolution.
  9. Ada: The provider said it was library music. Should we contact the composer or the performer?
  10. Ben: First verify who controls the relevant musical composition and recording rights. One provider may administer both, but we need evidence of authority and scope rather than guess from the track label.
  11. Ada: So permission for the song does not automatically tell us whether this exact recording is covered.
  12. Ben: Correct. Check the applicable master-use permission as well. We should map the intended audiovisual use to the permissions actually granted by the authorized parties.
  13. Ada: The campaign team wants to keep the date. Would using a shorter extract change the clearance question, or would we still need the wider grant?
  14. Ben: A shorter extract does not establish clearance. The release hold stays in place under our process until the necessary scope is confirmed or an appropriately cleared replacement is used.
  15. Ada: I can identify another cue, but I assume we must check that one too.
  16. Ben: Yes. A replacement cue is an alternative, not a shortcut around the review. Give the rights team the channels, countries, dates, and versions so it can assess the actual use.
  17. Ada: I will keep the creative export separate from the publication approval and include the relevant documents.
  18. Ben: Put the decision and remaining gaps in the clearance log. That helps the next reviewer see why the edit can be finished while release is still pending.
  19. Ada: We also need to prepare the music usage record for delivery.
  20. Ben: Prepare the cue sheet, but keep its purpose clear. It records the cue used; it does not replace the rights grant or turn the unresolved campaign scope into an approval.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Find the music license before release

The lesson's draft video uses music with unclear licensing documentation; the planned channels and territories are already recorded.

  1. Post-production producer: Could you locate the music license so the rights team can compare it with our distribution plan?
  2. Music supervisor: I can search the project records. Do you need the audio file or the licensing documentation?
  3. Post-production producer: The documentation, including the track identifier. We already know the planned channels and territories.
  4. Music supervisor: Would a purchase receipt be enough to clear the track for release?
  5. Post-production producer: I can't establish that. The rights reviewer needs to assess what the available documents actually cover.
  6. Music supervisor: Understood. I'll send the documents through the project folder and flag anything I cannot locate.
  7. Post-production producer: Could you also tell me when that search can be completed, rather than guessing a clearance date?
  8. Music supervisor: Yes. I can confirm the search timing after I check where the original purchase was recorded.
  9. Post-production producer: Until review is complete, I'll keep the music clearance marked unresolved in the release checklist.
  10. Music supervisor: I'll return with the records or a precise account of what remains missing.
An archive clip needs a new use review

Separate fictional case: a studio holds documentation for an archive clip in an internal training film and now proposes a public festival trailer.

  1. Archive producer: Please review the archive clip's rights for the proposed festival trailer; its existing documentation describes internal training.
  2. Clearance coordinator: Could you provide the exact clip reference and the proposed trailer use together?
  3. Archive producer: Yes. I'll send those with the existing license so you can compare the intended uses.
  4. Clearance coordinator: Do you already have confirmation that the public trailer falls within the existing grant?
  5. Archive producer: No. That's the point we need checked before treating the clip as cleared.
  6. Clearance coordinator: Then I may need to contact the rights holder after reviewing the wording.
  7. Archive producer: Please tell me whether that is necessary and what additional information they request.
  8. Clearance coordinator: I can start the document review today, but I cannot promise when an external response will arrive.
  9. Archive producer: That works. I'll keep an alternative edit available without presenting it as a cleared replacement.
  10. Clearance coordinator: I'll report the review status and any remaining permissions question before you lock the trailer.
A still image without a source trail

Separate fictional case: a magazine's digital teaser includes a historical photograph copied from a research deck; the original source and permission status are unknown.

  1. Digital editor: Could you trace the photograph in slide eight? We need its source before the teaser goes forward.
  2. Research producer: The research deck is where I found it, but that doesn't identify the original rights holder.
  3. Digital editor: Exactly. Please check the research notes rather than treating the deck as a license.
  4. Research producer: Should I ask design to remove the image while I investigate its clearance status?
  5. Digital editor: Ask them to prepare a version without it. We haven't established whether the proposed use is permitted.
  6. Research producer: What information should I bring back beyond a website link or the photographer's name?
  7. Digital editor: The source trail and any permission records, so our rights reviewer can assess the proposed digital use.
  8. Research producer: I can report what I find at our afternoon check-in; missing records may take longer.
  9. Digital editor: Please separate records found from conclusions about rights. We'll need review before making a release decision.
  10. Research producer: Understood. I'll trace the image and keep design informed without claiming the clearance is complete.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A music receipt is not the release clearance
  1. Read turns 1-10; name the channel, territory, and term gaps in the proposed use.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; separate composition, recording, and usage documentation.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Identify a second music-use gap

A document for cue C8 covers organic website use in France through 30 September. The proposed use adds paid social in Germany through 31 December. Expanded permission is unconfirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Editor: "The documented territory is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. France

France is the territory explicitly described in the available document.

2. Coordinator: "The new territory needing review is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Germany

Germany is added by the proposed use and is not established by the French territory description.

3. Editor: "The documented end date is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 30 September

Thirty September is the available permission's end date, not the proposed later date.

4. Coordinator: "The proposed end date is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 31 December

Thirty-one December is the intended campaign end, which needs rights review beyond the documented term.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Rights, Clearances, and Licensing
Original fictional case: Editor Ada has placed track M17 in a campaign video. A receipt is in the folder, but the available license document describes only organic use on the brand website in the United States from 1 June to 31 August. The planned campaign also includes paid social in the United States and Canada through 30 November. Clearance coordinator Ben has not verified the necessary composition and recording permissions for that expanded use. Under this production's release process, the video stays on hold until the required scope is confirmed or an appropriately cleared replacement is used.
Language workshop: A music receipt is not the release clearance
Communication goal: Compare intended music use with documented permission and explain a publication hold precisely.
Language guidance: The composition and the specific recording are distinct rights subjects. A single provider may administer both, but the team must verify its authority and the actual grant. Do not assume a receipt establishes every required permission.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We are reviewing music track M17. / The receipt does not establish the complete license scope. / The document describes organic website use in the United States. / The listed term runs from 1 June to 31 August. / The campaign also includes paid social and Canada through November. / Channel, territory, and term all need confirmation. / We need the relevant composition and recording permissions checked. / Confirm that the licensor controls the rights it claims to grant. / The video remains on hold under our release process. / A shorter extract does not automatically solve the clearance issue. / We can seek the expanded rights or assess a cleared replacement. / The replacement also needs its intended uses verified. / Link the license and scope decision in the clearance log. / A cue sheet documents the music; it does not grant the rights. / The creative edit can be ready while public release remains blocked. / Release follows confirmed scope, not an assumed meaning of royalty-free.
Vocabulary: clearance: Confirmation of permissions required for a particular use. / license scope: The boundaries of a granted permission. / musical composition: The underlying music and any lyrics. / sound recording: A particular recorded performance or production of sound. / synchronization license: Permission concerning music used in timed relation to visuals. / master-use permission: Permission to use a particular sound recording. / rights holder: The party controlling a relevant legal right. / music publisher: An entity administering relevant rights in musical works. / record label: A company involved in recorded music and potentially controlling recording rights. / chain of title: Records showing ownership or transfer of relevant rights. / territory: The geographical area covered by permission. / license term: The period during which the permitted use is authorized. / paid media: Placement or distribution supported by advertising spend. / organic use: Distribution without paid amplification in the specified context. / usage restriction: A condition limiting how material may be used. / cue sheet: A record of music used in a production and relevant details. / temporary track: Music used provisionally while the final choice or clearance is pending. / library music: Music offered through a catalog under applicable licensing terms. / royalty-free: A licensing description whose actual payment and use terms must be checked. / public domain: A status in which relevant copyright protection does not apply in the jurisdiction concerned. / release hold: A block on publication pending a required condition. / clearance log: A record of rights requests, documents, decisions, and gaps. / rights extension: An agreed expansion of the period or scope of permission. / replacement cue: A substitute music selection for a production.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; name the channel, territory, and term gaps in the proposed use. Swap roles for turns 11-20; separate composition, recording, and usage documentation. Complete the transfer while keeping documented permission separate from proposed use.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Talent, Contracts, and ApprovalsA delivery target arrives before the review window ends+

Your goal

Coordinate version-specific talent approval and explain a schedule conflict without assuming silence means consent.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A delivery target arrives before the review window ends

For campaign F29, the fictional talent agreement allows 48 elapsed hours after confirmed receipt of the complete review package. The package includes the main film and vertical version. It is received Monday at 10:00, so the full window ends Wednesday at 10:00; all times use the same time zone. The delivery calendar targets Tuesday at 17:00. Approval may arrive earlier, but none has yet been received and silence is not deemed approval under the stated terms. Coordinator Mina must agree submission ownership and a realistic delivery message with producer Joel.

Mina: Talent coordinator / Joel: Producer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When does the full review window end?

Answer and explanation

B. Wednesday at 10:00

Forty-eight elapsed hours after Monday at ten ends Wednesday at ten in the same time zone.

2. What must the complete package include here?

Answer and explanation

A. The main film and vertical version

The fictional terms explicitly require both versions in the complete review package.

3. What does silence mean under these terms?

Answer and explanation

A. It is not deemed approval.

The briefing expressly states that silence is not deemed approval under this agreement.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Forty-eight elapsed hours is not the same as two business days unless the agreement makes them equivalent. This case expressly uses elapsed hours and one time zone. The full window ends Wednesday at 10:00.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
talent agreement
A contract governing a performer's engagement and relevant uses.review the talent agreement
approval right
A contractual right to review and approve specified material.respect the approval right
review window
The time allowed for a specified assessment.calculate the review window
complete submission
Delivery of all materials required to start the stated review.confirm a complete submission
receipt confirmation
Evidence that the relevant recipient received the materials.obtain receipt confirmation
elapsed hours
Continuous hours passing from a stated starting point.count elapsed hours
business days
Working days as defined by the relevant agreement or process.define business days
deemed approval
Approval treated as occurring under a specified contractual condition.check deemed-approval wording
version identifier
A label distinguishing one specific file or revision.preserve the version identifier
approval tracker
A record of submissions, recipients, deadlines, and decisions.maintain an approval tracker
consolidated notes
Feedback combined into a coordinated set.request consolidated notes
revision round
A defined cycle of feedback and changes.schedule a revision round
final cut
A specified completed edit, subject to the actual approval arrangement.identify the final cut
picture lock
The stage at which picture editing is fixed for subsequent work.confirm picture lock
talent representative
A person authorized to act for a performer in relevant matters.contact the talent representative
usage buyout
A negotiated payment arrangement for specified usage rights.define the usage buyout
residual
A payment for qualifying further use under applicable terms.check residual obligations
exclusivity clause
A term restricting specified competing engagements or uses.review the exclusivity clause
likeness
A person's recognizable image or representation.confirm likeness rights
voice usage
The permitted use of a person's recorded or synthesized voice.define voice usage
digital replica
A digital representation of a person's likeness or voice.assess digital-replica terms
reshoot
Filming material again after the original shoot.scope a reshoot
pickup shot
Additional footage captured to complete or supplement an edit.plan a pickup shot
approval record
Evidence of the actual decision on specified material.retain the approval record

A Tuesday target does not shorten the agreed review window. Earlier approval is possible, not guaranteed. A changed version may require further review under the actual terms; approval of one file should not be generalized to every export.

C Notice the language

Treat approval as a documented event

Creative teams often use casual phrases such as they seemed happy or we should be fine. Those impressions do not establish approval of a particular version. State who received what, when the review began, and what decision is still outstanding.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate the governing term
The agreement allows forty-eight elapsed hours.
Confirm the start event
The complete package was received Monday at 10:00.
List the required versions
The package contains the main film and vertical version.
Calculate the window
The full review period ends Wednesday at 10:00.
Identify the conflict
Tuesday's delivery target comes before that window ends.
Keep an early response conditional
Approval may arrive earlier, but it has not arrived yet.
Avoid assumed consent
Silence is not deemed approval under these terms.
Name the submission owner
Mina will coordinate the package and receipt confirmation.
Track the actual file
Record the version identifier with every approval.
Request a faster review
Can the representative agree to an accelerated review?
Preserve the answer boundary
A request for acceleration is not acceptance of it.
Coordinate feedback
Please return one consolidated set of notes where the process permits.
Separate creative and contractual status
Picture lock does not itself establish talent approval.
Check a changed export
Confirm whether this revision requires a new review.
Keep the client message accurate
We are awaiting the specified approval before final delivery.
Close the record
Retain the decision, version, recipient, and timestamp together.
6 language and precision notes
After confirmed receipt
Identifies the event that starts the clock.
Elapsed
Means continuous passing hours under the stated terms.
Comes before
Shows the calendar conflict without assuming no early response is possible.
May ... but
Preserves a possibility while stating that it is not yet an event.
Under these terms
Limits the rule to the fictional agreement, not every talent contract.
Not acceptance
Separates asking for a change from securing it.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which client update is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Approval is pending; the full window ends Wednesday at 10:00, after Tuesday's target.

The actual window and pending decision must remain visible despite the earlier delivery target.

2. Which calculation follows the stated terms?

Answer and explanation

B. Monday 10:00 plus 48 elapsed hours equals Wednesday 10:00.

The case defines continuous elapsed hours starting from confirmed complete-package receipt.

3. Which action is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. Ask whether an accelerated review can be agreed without assuming acceptance.

An acceleration request is legitimate, but the requested change requires an actual agreement or decision.

4. What should the approval record identify?

Answer and explanation

A. The particular version, decision, recipient, and timestamp

Version-specific evidence prevents approval of one submission from being applied indiscriminately to another.

E Conversations

A delivery target arrives before the review window ends

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mina: Talent coordinator / Joel: Producer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • approval tracker
  • Picture lock
  • talent representative
  • talent agreement
  • elapsed hours
  • deemed approval
  • approval record
  • complete submission
  • consolidated notes
  • review window
  1. Joel
    The client wants F29 Tuesday at five. I see Monday's submission in the tracker, but no decision. How much of the review window is still left?
  2. Mina
    Approval is still pending. The 1 allows forty-eight elapsed hours after confirmed receipt of the complete package. We received confirmation for Monday at ten, but no approval decision.
  3. Joel
    Was that package complete, including the vertical version?
  4. Mina
    Yes. The 2 contained the main film and vertical version. I will keep the file identifiers with the receipt so we know exactly what the representative is reviewing.
  5. Joel
    Forty-eight hours takes us to Wednesday at ten, using the same time zone.
  6. Mina
    Correct. Those are 3, not an assumed business-day calculation. The calendar needs to show that full window, even though our preferred delivery time is earlier.
  7. Joel
    They might answer on Tuesday. I do not want to tell the client that Wednesday is certain if an earlier response is possible.
  8. Mina
    Agreed. The 4 ends Wednesday, but approval could arrive sooner. We should communicate the dependency without pretending either the early response or a delay is already decided.
  9. Joel
    Could we treat no response by Tuesday afternoon as consent?
  10. Mina
    No. The stated terms do not provide 5. A deadline on our internal calendar does not create that mechanism. Silence cannot be turned into a positive decision here.
  11. Joel
    Then please ask whether the representative can accommodate an accelerated review.
  12. Mina
    I will contact the 6. I will distinguish our request from their answer and record any agreed change. Until then, the original window remains the planning basis.
  13. Joel
    If we receive notes, I would like one coordinated list instead of conflicting messages from several people.
  14. Mina
    I can request 7 where the process permits. We will still preserve who gave the feedback and what it concerns, rather than merge away a substantive disagreement.
  15. Joel
    The editor says picture is locked. Does that make the remaining review only a formality?
  16. Mina
    No. 8 describes an editing stage. It does not cancel the approval right. If a requested change affects the edit, we must assess it under the actual agreement and schedule.
  17. Joel
    Can you own the submission and follow-up? I need one place to check which files were received and whether the decision covers them.
  18. Mina
    I will own the 9. If a file changes, I will check whether it needs a new review and keep that separate from any decision on the previous version.
  19. Joel
    I will tell the client approval is pending, the full window ends Wednesday at ten, and we have requested an earlier response.
  20. Mina
    That is accurate. Once we have an 10, we can update the delivery status. Until then, a requested acceleration and a locked edit must not be reported as completed approval.

Answers and explanations
  1. talent agreement Turn 2. Talent agreement supplies the actual review terms; the delivery target does not replace those terms or establish approval.
  2. complete submission Turn 4. Complete submission includes both the main film and vertical version, satisfying the stated package requirement for starting this review.
  3. elapsed hours Turn 6. Elapsed hours run continuously from Monday at ten to Wednesday at ten under the fictional agreement.
  4. review window Turn 8. Review window is the allowed assessment period; it does not prevent an earlier response, but no early approval is guaranteed.
  5. deemed approval Turn 10. Deemed approval would arise only under specified terms; this agreement expressly does not treat silence as approval.
  6. talent representative Turn 12. Talent representative is the appropriate contact for the review request; asking for acceleration does not establish its acceptance.
  7. consolidated notes Turn 14. Consolidated notes combine feedback into a coordinated set, helping the team plan a revision without inventing approval.
  8. Picture lock Turn 16. Picture lock fixes the picture-editing stage for subsequent work; it does not itself satisfy the separate talent approval requirement.
  9. approval tracker Turn 18. Approval tracker records submissions, versions, recipients, timing, and decisions so responsibility remains clear throughout the process.
  10. approval record Turn 20. Approval record documents the actual decision on identified material; it replaces speculation based on deadlines or informal impressions.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Who sends the talent approval package?

The lesson's talent agreement includes an approval step, but the calendar omits the submission owner and timing.

  1. Production coordinator: The talent agreement includes an approval step. Who is responsible for submitting the material?
  2. Talent liaison: The calendar doesn't identify that person, so I cannot confirm who owns the submission.
  3. Production coordinator: Could you help establish the owner before we add a review milestone to the schedule?
  4. Talent liaison: Yes. We should also clarify what material the approval right covers rather than sending everything automatically.
  5. Production coordinator: Does the current calendar say when the reviewer needs to receive the package?
  6. Talent liaison: No. Submission timing is missing too, and I don't want to invent a turnaround period.
  7. Production coordinator: Then should we ask the agreement owner to confirm scope, submission contact, and timing?
  8. Talent liaison: Exactly. My role in communicating with talent doesn't itself resolve those contractual details.
  9. Production coordinator: I'll record the unanswered questions and keep the release milestone conditional on the required approvals.
  10. Talent liaison: Once the agreement owner responds, let's read back the responsibilities together before updating the calendar.
Which photograph did the agent approve?

Separate fictional case: an agent's email approves portrait file P4; a publicity folder contains P4 and a differently cropped file P4-square.

  1. Publicity producer: The agent approved P4. Does that also cover the square crop in our publicity folder?
  2. Talent coordinator: The email names P4 only. I can't assume it includes the separately labeled square version.
  3. Publicity producer: Could you clarify whether the approval concerns this exact image treatment or the underlying photograph?
  4. Talent coordinator: I'll ask using both filenames and thumbnails, so the question is specific.
  5. Publicity producer: We should avoid describing the talent's likeness as fully approved across every promotional asset, then?
  6. Talent coordinator: Correct. We have an approval for a named file, not a blanket statement about all assets.
  7. Publicity producer: Would it help if I identify which placement needs the square crop?
  8. Talent coordinator: Yes. Include that intended use so the reviewer knows what we are asking them to consider.
  9. Publicity producer: I'll keep the square asset pending and send a clear comparison without altering either file.
  10. Talent coordinator: I'll seek clarification and attach the response to the exact version before publicity proceeds.
A signed release is not a recorded review

Separate fictional case: a training-video project has a signed contributor release and a project checklist with an unfilled interview-review item; the item's purpose is unclear.

  1. Post-production coordinator: The contributor release is signed, but the interview-review box is blank. What does that review mean here?
  2. Producer: I don't know whether it concerns factual accuracy or an approval right. The label is unclear.
  3. Post-production coordinator: Could we check the agreed arrangement before treating the signed release as completion of both steps?
  4. Producer: Yes. Those records may answer different questions, and we shouldn't merge them without checking.
  5. Post-production coordinator: Who can clarify the purpose of the review item and who is expected to respond?
  6. Producer: I'll ask the colleague who created the checklist and compare their explanation with the project agreement.
  7. Post-production coordinator: Should the editor send the interview directly to the contributor in the meantime?
  8. Producer: Let's first confirm the submission process and material required. An unnecessary send could create more confusion.
  9. Post-production coordinator: I'll leave the review item open and label the signed release separately in the tracker.
  10. Producer: Good. We'll confirm what remains required before telling the editor that the interview can be finalized.

Track a second review window

A fictional agreement allows 24 elapsed hours after complete receipt, with no deemed approval. The package is received Thursday at 14:00 in the same time zone used for all deadlines. The client target is Friday at 09:00; approval is pending.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "Receipt was confirmed on ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Thursday at 14:00

Thursday at fourteen hundred is the stated event that starts the review clock.

2. Producer: "The full window ends on ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Friday at 14:00

Twenty-four elapsed hours after Thursday at fourteen hundred ends Friday at fourteen hundred.

3. Coordinator: "The earlier client target is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Friday at 09:00

Friday at nine is the desired delivery target, not the end of the agreed review window.

4. Producer: "Silence is not deemed ___."

Answer and explanation

D. approval

The supplied terms expressly exclude treating silence as an approval decision.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

REFERENCE
Course: Media and Entertainment English
Professional audience: producers, production managers, creative executives, rights coordinators, distribution teams, talent managers, marketing staff, and media operations professionals
Scope: All productions, clients, performers, incidents, figures, and contract terms are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not legal advice or production-safety procedures. Rights, labor conditions, disclosures, and approvals depend on the actual agreements, uses, jurisdiction, and current requirements. Lesson 04: Talent, Contracts, and Approvals
Original fictional case: For campaign F29, the fictional talent agreement allows 48 elapsed hours after confirmed receipt of the complete review package. The package includes the main film and vertical version. It is received Monday at 10:00, so the full window ends Wednesday at 10:00; all times use the same time zone. The delivery calendar targets Tuesday at 17:00. Approval may arrive earlier, but none has yet been received and silence is not deemed approval under the stated terms. Coordinator Mina must agree submission ownership and a realistic delivery message with producer Joel.
Language workshop: A delivery target arrives before the review window ends
Communication goal: Coordinate version-specific talent approval and explain a schedule conflict without assuming silence means consent.
Language guidance: Forty-eight elapsed hours is not the same as two business days unless the agreement makes them equivalent. This case expressly uses elapsed hours and one time zone. The full window ends Wednesday at 10:00.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The agreement allows forty-eight elapsed hours. / The complete package was received Monday at 10:00. / The package contains the main film and vertical version. / The full review period ends Wednesday at 10:00. / Tuesday's delivery target comes before that window ends. / Approval may arrive earlier, but it has not arrived yet. / Silence is not deemed approval under these terms. / Mina will coordinate the package and receipt confirmation. / Record the version identifier with every approval. / Can the representative agree to an accelerated review? / A request for acceleration is not acceptance of it. / Please return one consolidated set of notes where the process permits. / Picture lock does not itself establish talent approval. / Confirm whether this revision requires a new review. / We are awaiting the specified approval before final delivery. / Retain the decision, version, recipient, and timestamp together.
Vocabulary: talent agreement: A contract governing a performer's engagement and relevant uses. / approval right: A contractual right to review and approve specified material. / review window: The time allowed for a specified assessment. / complete submission: Delivery of all materials required to start the stated review. / receipt confirmation: Evidence that the relevant recipient received the materials. / elapsed hours: Continuous hours passing from a stated starting point. / business days: Working days as defined by the relevant agreement or process. / deemed approval: Approval treated as occurring under a specified contractual condition. / version identifier: A label distinguishing one specific file or revision. / approval tracker: A record of submissions, recipients, deadlines, and decisions. / consolidated notes: Feedback combined into a coordinated set. / revision round: A defined cycle of feedback and changes. / final cut: A specified completed edit, subject to the actual approval arrangement. / picture lock: The stage at which picture editing is fixed for subsequent work. / talent representative: A person authorized to act for a performer in relevant matters. / usage buyout: A negotiated payment arrangement for specified usage rights. / residual: A payment for qualifying further use under applicable terms. / exclusivity clause: A term restricting specified competing engagements or uses. / likeness: A person's recognizable image or representation. / voice usage: The permitted use of a person's recorded or synthesized voice. / digital replica: A digital representation of a person's likeness or voice. / reshoot: Filming material again after the original shoot. / pickup shot: Additional footage captured to complete or supplement an edit. / approval record: Evidence of the actual decision on specified material.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; calculate the elapsed-hour window from complete receipt. Swap roles for turns 11-20; request acceleration without reporting it as agreed. Complete the transfer and distinguish the client target from the review deadline.
Partner: Mina: Talent coordinator / Joel: Producer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A fictional agreement allows 24 elapsed hours after complete receipt, with no deemed approval. The package is received Thursday at 14:00 in the same time zone used for all deadlines. The client target is Friday at 09:00; approval is pending.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Joel: Can you own the submission and follow-up? I need one place to check which files were received and whether the decision covers them.
Mina: I will own the approval tracker. If a file changes, I will check whether it needs a new review and keep that separate from any decision on the previous version.
Joel: I will tell the client approval is pending, the full window ends Wednesday at ten, and we have requested an earlier response.
Mina: That is accurate. Once we have an approval record, we can update the delivery status. Until then, a requested acceleration and a locked edit must not be reported as completed approval.
END REFERENCE
05Distribution and WindowingThe larger fee also sells a broader restriction+

Your goal

Compare distribution offers across fee, rights, territory, term, and exclusivity without inventing net value.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The larger fee also sells a broader restriction

For documentary North Line, Offer A proposes a flat $30,000 fee for exclusive worldwide video-on-demand rights for 24 months. Offer B proposes a flat $22,000 fee for nonexclusive advertising-supported video-on-demand rights in the United States for six months. Both proposed terms would begin on the same date, but neither deal is signed. Delivery costs, taxes, and other deductions are not supplied; available underlying rights still require verification. Sales lead Priya and producer Mason must compare the offers without choosing solely by fee or assuming both can be accepted unchanged.

Priya: Distribution sales lead / Mason: Producer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which offer has the larger headline fee?

Answer and explanation

B. A, at $30,000

Offer A explicitly states thirty thousand dollars, exceeding B's twenty-two thousand.

2. Which offer is narrower in stated territory and duration?

Answer and explanation

D. B: United States, six months

Offer B is limited to the United States and six months under the supplied terms.

3. Can both be accepted unchanged without examining overlap?

Answer and explanation

C. No; A's exclusive rights overlap B's proposed use.

A's worldwide exclusive video-on-demand scope includes the United States advertising-supported use proposed by B.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The $8,000 fee difference does not establish an $8,000 difference in profit or overall value. A grants broader and longer exclusive rights. Costs, deductions, existing commitments, and future opportunities require their own review.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
distribution window
A defined period or sequence for particular release rights.plan a distribution window
territorial rights
Rights limited to specified geographic areas.define territorial rights
exclusive license
Permission reserving the specified licensed use to the licensee under its terms.negotiate an exclusive license
nonexclusive license
Permission that does not itself reserve the use to one licensee.offer a nonexclusive license
video on demand (VOD)
Content available for viewing at a user's chosen time.license VOD rights
advertising-supported VOD (AVOD)
On-demand viewing funded through advertising under the service model.compare AVOD offers
subscription VOD (SVOD)
On-demand viewing under a subscription service model.define SVOD rights
transactional VOD (TVOD)
On-demand viewing paid for per rental or purchase.negotiate TVOD terms
free ad-supported streaming TV (FAST)
Scheduled streaming channels available without subscription and supported by ads.evaluate FAST distribution
holdback
A contractual restriction delaying another release or use.review the holdback
carve-out
An explicit exception to a broader rights restriction.negotiate a carve-out
rights reversion
Return or expiration of granted rights under the agreement.confirm rights reversion
license fee
Payment for the specified permission.compare license fees
minimum guarantee
A contractually specified minimum payment, often linked to later accounting.review the minimum guarantee
revenue share
An agreed division of defined income.calculate revenue share
recoupment
Recovery of specified amounts from defined revenues.examine recoupment terms
distribution fee
An amount charged for distribution services under the agreement.verify the distribution fee
delivery specification
Technical and material requirements for supplied content.check delivery specifications
localization
Adapting content for a language or market.price localization
caption file
A file carrying timed text for accessible or translated presentation.deliver the caption file
geoblocking
Restricting access based on geographic location.verify geoblocking requirements
avails
Information about the rights and periods available for licensing.update the avails
rights grid
A table mapping rights by territory, medium, and period.maintain the rights grid
net receipts
Income remaining after the deductions defined by the agreement.define net receipts

Nonexclusive means B would not itself demand exclusivity; it does not override exclusivity granted to A. Because both proposed terms start together, A overlaps B. Any carve-out would require an actual negotiated agreement.

C Notice the language

Describe the restriction as part of the price

A headline fee can dominate a fast sales conversation. Put the rights granted beside the money received. Questions about exclusivity are not necessarily resistance to a deal; they identify which future choices the offer would remove.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Start with the headline
Offer A pays $30,000 and Offer B pays $22,000.
Add the missing context
The larger fee comes with broader exclusive rights.
State A's duration
A proposes twenty-four months.
State B's duration
B proposes six months.
Name the territorial difference
A is worldwide; B is United States only.
Separate the media scopes
A covers VOD broadly, while B specifies AVOD.
Preserve proposal status
Neither offer is signed.
Identify the overlap
A's exclusive scope overlaps B's proposed use.
Reject an automatic combination
We cannot simply accept both unchanged.
Explore a negotiated route
We could ask whether A will agree to an AVOD carve-out.
Keep the request conditional
A requested carve-out is not a granted exception.
Verify available rights
Check the underlying rights and existing commitments first.
Avoid a net-value claim
We do not yet have the delivery costs or deductions.
Make definitions explicit
Net receipts depend on the actual agreement.
Record the comparison
Put fee, term, territory, media, and exclusivity on one rights grid.
Close without premature selection
The fee comparison is clear; the full commercial decision is still open.
6 language and precision notes
Comes with
Connects the payment to the restriction received in exchange.
Proposes
Marks an offer as unsigned rather than already effective.
Broadly ... while
Contrasts a wider category with a narrower specified use.
Cannot simply
Challenges an unsupported shortcut without excluding negotiation.
Will agree to
Identifies the other party's required consent.
Depend on
Prevents a contract term from being treated as having one universal definition.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison captures the main trade-off?

Answer and explanation

A. A pays more for broader, longer exclusive rights; B pays less for narrower, shorter nonexclusive rights.

The complete comparison includes scope, duration, and exclusivity alongside the headline fee.

2. Why does B's nonexclusive wording not solve the conflict?

Answer and explanation

B. It does not override the exclusivity A would receive in the overlapping scope.

A separate nonexclusive grant cannot by itself defeat an overlapping exclusive commitment to another party.

3. Which statement is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Net receipts are exactly $30,000 under A.

The missing deductions and delivery costs prevent treating the headline fee as established net receipts.

4. Which next step is a proposal rather than an agreement?

Answer and explanation

B. Ask A to consider a defined AVOD carve-out.

Asking for a carve-out starts a negotiation but does not establish acceptance or available rights.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

The larger fee also sells a broader restriction

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: Distribution sales lead / Mason: Producer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • nonexclusive license
  • exclusive license
  • distribution window
  • delivery specification
  • carve-out
  • net receipts
  • Advertising-supported VOD
  • rights grid
  • rights reversion
  • license fee
  1. Mason
    A is eight thousand higher. Before I recommend it, what would we be giving up that is not obvious from the fee column?
  2. Priya
    The 1 is larger, but the rights package is different. A wants worldwide exclusive VOD rights for twenty-four months. B wants United States AVOD rights for six months, nonexclusively.
  3. Mason
    So B leaves more future options open, at least under its own proposed restriction.
  4. Priya
    Yes, but we still need the 2 and underlying permissions checked. We should not promise that every unmentioned right is freely available when we have not verified existing commitments.
  5. Mason
    Could we take both fees? B is nonexclusive, so it sounds compatible with another service.
  6. Priya
    B's 3 does not cancel A's exclusivity. Both offers begin on the same date, and A's worldwide VOD scope includes the United States use that B wants.
  7. Mason
    Then accepting A unchanged would conflict with the proposed B grant.
  8. Priya
    Correct. An 4 has to be assessed across medium, territory, and time. We cannot avoid the overlap simply by putting the proposals on different lines in a spreadsheet.
  9. Mason
    Could we ask A to leave advertising-supported viewing out of its exclusivity?
  10. Priya
    We could negotiate a 5. That is a possible negotiating route, not a term already granted. The exact AVOD scope and any other restrictions would need clear wording.
  11. Mason
    I also want to know when we could offer the film elsewhere after A's period.
  12. Priya
    Check 6 and any holdbacks in the actual draft. A headline twenty-four-month term does not tell us every end-of-term obligation or permitted next release.
  13. Mason
    The summary labels the thirty thousand net income, but delivery and localization are still unpriced. Can we fix that label before it reaches the producer?
  14. Priya
    Then it should not label that 7. We only have the flat headline fee here. Delivery costs, taxes, and other deductions are not supplied, so the net comparison is incomplete.
  15. Mason
    B specifically wants advertising-supported on-demand rights. Does that automatically include scheduled free streaming channels?
  16. Priya
    Do not assume so. 8 names its stated service model. A scheduled FAST channel is a different format that needs its own rights description in the agreement.
  17. Mason
    We should ask each buyer for its technical package before estimating delivery effort.
  18. Priya
    Yes. The 9 may include versions, captions, and localization. Those requirements affect cost and readiness even when the headline fee is fixed.
  19. Mason
    I will keep both offers unsigned in the comparison and show their actual restrictions, not just the money.
  20. Priya
    Good. Then we can assess the 10 and negotiate from a complete picture. A's higher fee is established; its overall superiority is not established by that number alone.

Answers and explanations
  1. license fee Turn 2. License fee is the payment for defined permission; the larger amount must be read beside the rights requested.
  2. rights grid Turn 4. Rights grid maps medium, territory, period, and exclusivity, making both the proposed grants and existing limitations visible.
  3. nonexclusive license Turn 6. Nonexclusive license does not demand sole use for B, but it cannot override exclusive rights granted separately to A.
  4. exclusive license Turn 8. Exclusive license reserves its specified use under the agreement, creating the overlap problem with B's proposed simultaneous grant.
  5. carve-out Turn 10. Carve-out is an explicit exception to a broader restriction; it would need A's actual agreement rather than an internal assumption.
  6. rights reversion Turn 12. Rights reversion concerns when granted rights return or expire under the actual agreement, which must be verified rather than assumed.
  7. net receipts Turn 14. Net receipts require the agreement's defined deductions and relevant costs; the headline fee alone does not establish that amount.
  8. Advertising-supported VOD Turn 16. Advertising-supported VOD refers to on-demand viewing funded by advertising and should not be silently expanded to every ad-supported distribution format.
  9. delivery specification Turn 18. Delivery specification defines required materials and technical formats, allowing the team to assess work rather than assume equal delivery burdens.
  10. distribution window Turn 20. Distribution window organizes the period and sequence of permitted releases, connecting the deal to future exploitation choices.
Read the complete conversation

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Compare what the fee buys

The lesson's distribution proposals differ in exclusivity and territory, but their summary shows only fees; exact terms are not supplied here.

  1. Sales agent: The fee comparison makes one distribution proposal look stronger, but the rights packages are different.
  2. Producer: Which differences should I hear before deciding whether the higher fee is more attractive?
  3. Sales agent: We need to compare territories, exclusivity periods, and the rights window alongside the fee.
  4. Producer: Could a larger payment mean giving up opportunities on other platforms for longer?
  5. Sales agent: It could, depending on the actual exclusivity terms. The summary doesn't show enough to assess that.
  6. Producer: Then calling one offer better value now would hide a tradeoff we haven't examined.
  7. Sales agent: Exactly. Both proposals need to appear against the same criteria, even where their terms differ.
  8. Producer: Please include which future distribution choices each proposal might limit, subject to the agreement review.
  9. Sales agent: I'll request a complete comparison and flag any windowing details that remain unclear.
  10. Producer: We'll discuss the fees with those conditions visible, rather than treating unlike rights packages as equivalent.
A short exclusive window or a longer nonexclusive run

Separate fictional case: a short film receives two offers for the same territory: six months exclusive at 8,000 units or twelve months nonexclusive at 6,000 units; other terms remain unreviewed.

  1. Distribution manager: Both offers cover the same territory. One pays eight thousand for six months of exclusivity.
  2. Film producer: The other pays six thousand for twelve months without exclusivity. Does the shorter window clearly win?
  3. Distribution manager: It pays more, but exclusivity may limit parallel placements during those six months.
  4. Film producer: Whereas the nonexclusive proposal preserves more placement flexibility, although the fee is lower?
  5. Distribution manager: That is the stated distinction. We still need to review the remaining rights terms.
  6. Film producer: Could we simply double the six-month fee to compare an annual figure?
  7. Distribution manager: No. That would assume an identical second deal exists after the first rights window closes.
  8. Film producer: Then show the actual payments and durations, not an invented annual earnings forecast.
  9. Distribution manager: I'll also identify how each proposal fits our intended windowing sequence, with unresolved terms marked.
  10. Film producer: Good. We'll weigh flexibility against the larger guaranteed fee only after that review.
Download access versus festival access

Separate fictional case: a documentary team compares two proposed first releases: a paid download platform with no minimum guarantee and a festival package with a fixed screening fee; both have unconfirmed later-window restrictions.

  1. Documentary producer: The download platform offers wider access, while the festival package includes a fixed screening fee.
  2. Distribution adviser: Those are different benefits. Which criteria matter most for the first rights window?
  3. Documentary producer: Audience access and predictable income. We should consider both, rather than comparing only projected sales.
  4. Distribution adviser: Agreed. The download proposal has no minimum guarantee, so projected revenue is not a confirmed payment.
  5. Documentary producer: And the festival fee is fixed, but that doesn't tell us how many people will attend.
  6. Distribution adviser: Exactly. We also haven't confirmed either proposal's restrictions on later platform distribution.
  7. Documentary producer: Would it be premature to promise a download release immediately after the festival screenings?
  8. Distribution adviser: Yes. We need the windowing conditions before presenting that sequence as available.
  9. Documentary producer: Let's compare access, payment certainty, and later-window flexibility with the missing terms clearly identified.
  10. Distribution adviser: I'll obtain those terms, then we can discuss the tradeoff without turning estimates into guarantees.

Compare another pair of offers

Offer C pays $12,000 for exclusive UK SVOD rights for 12 months. Offer D pays $9,000 for nonexclusive French AVOD rights for three months. Neither is signed; no cost figures are supplied.

Answer and explanation

C. 12,000

Twelve thousand dollars is the proposed fee, not an established net-income figure.

2. Sales lead: "D's territory is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. France

France is the stated geographic scope of the second offer.

3. Producer: "C's proposed term is ___ months."

Answer and explanation

A. 12

Twelve months is the duration of the UK SVOD proposal.

4. Sales lead: "D's proposed term is ___ months."

Answer and explanation

A. three

Three months is the duration of the French AVOD proposal, distinct from C's longer term.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 05: Distribution and Windowing
Original fictional case: For documentary North Line, Offer A proposes a flat $30,000 fee for exclusive worldwide video-on-demand rights for 24 months. Offer B proposes a flat $22,000 fee for nonexclusive advertising-supported video-on-demand rights in the United States for six months. Both proposed terms would begin on the same date, but neither deal is signed. Delivery costs, taxes, and other deductions are not supplied; available underlying rights still require verification. Sales lead Priya and producer Mason must compare the offers without choosing solely by fee or assuming both can be accepted unchanged.
Language workshop: The larger fee also sells a broader restriction
Communication goal: Compare distribution offers across fee, rights, territory, term, and exclusivity without inventing net value.
Language guidance: The $8,000 fee difference does not establish an $8,000 difference in profit or overall value. A grants broader and longer exclusive rights. Costs, deductions, existing commitments, and future opportunities require their own review.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Offer A pays $30,000 and Offer B pays $22,000. / The larger fee comes with broader exclusive rights. / A proposes twenty-four months. / B proposes six months. / A is worldwide; B is United States only. / A covers VOD broadly, while B specifies AVOD. / Neither offer is signed. / A's exclusive scope overlaps B's proposed use. / We cannot simply accept both unchanged. / We could ask whether A will agree to an AVOD carve-out. / A requested carve-out is not a granted exception. / Check the underlying rights and existing commitments first. / We do not yet have the delivery costs or deductions. / Net receipts depend on the actual agreement. / Put fee, term, territory, media, and exclusivity on one rights grid. / The fee comparison is clear; the full commercial decision is still open.
Vocabulary: distribution window: A defined period or sequence for particular release rights. / territorial rights: Rights limited to specified geographic areas. / exclusive license: Permission reserving the specified licensed use to the licensee under its terms. / nonexclusive license: Permission that does not itself reserve the use to one licensee. / video on demand (VOD): Content available for viewing at a user's chosen time. / advertising-supported VOD (AVOD): On-demand viewing funded through advertising under the service model. / subscription VOD (SVOD): On-demand viewing under a subscription service model. / transactional VOD (TVOD): On-demand viewing paid for per rental or purchase. / free ad-supported streaming TV (FAST): Scheduled streaming channels available without subscription and supported by ads. / holdback: A contractual restriction delaying another release or use. / carve-out: An explicit exception to a broader rights restriction. / rights reversion: Return or expiration of granted rights under the agreement. / license fee: Payment for the specified permission. / minimum guarantee: A contractually specified minimum payment, often linked to later accounting. / revenue share: An agreed division of defined income. / recoupment: Recovery of specified amounts from defined revenues. / distribution fee: An amount charged for distribution services under the agreement. / delivery specification: Technical and material requirements for supplied content. / localization: Adapting content for a language or market. / caption file: A file carrying timed text for accessible or translated presentation. / geoblocking: Restricting access based on geographic location. / avails: Information about the rights and periods available for licensing. / rights grid: A table mapping rights by territory, medium, and period. / net receipts: Income remaining after the deductions defined by the agreement.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; compare fee, territory, term, medium, and exclusivity. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish a requested carve-out from an agreed one. Complete the transfer without labeling either headline fee as net income.
Partner: Priya: Distribution sales lead / Mason: Producer

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Audience Metrics and PerformanceMore completed plays, but a lower completion rate+

Your goal

Give a balanced performance readout using distinct counts, rates, and campaign objectives.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

More completed plays, but a lower completion rate

Two comparable reporting periods cover the same 30-second video on one platform. Period A records 1,000 eligible starts, 800 complete plays, and estimated reach of 900 accounts. Period B records 2,000 eligible starts, 1,200 complete plays, and estimated reach of 1,500 accounts. A complete play reaches the end; completion rate is complete plays divided by eligible starts. Reach and starts are different measures. Spend and business-outcome data are not supplied. Analyst Felix and account director Dana must replace an unqualified success claim with a precise account of the gains and decline.

Dana: Account director / Felix: Audience analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is Period B's completion rate?

Answer and explanation

B. 60%

Twelve hundred complete plays divided by two thousand eligible starts equals sixty percent.

2. What happened to complete-play count?

Answer and explanation

A. It rose from 800 to 1,200.

The briefing supplies eight hundred complete plays in A and twelve hundred in B.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. Spend and business-outcome data

The record explicitly leaves spending and business outcomes outside the available figures.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Complete plays increased by 400, or 50%, while completion rate fell from 80% to 60%, a decline of 20 percentage points. A larger audience can produce more completions even when a smaller share finishes.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
reach
The estimated or measured distinct audience under a stated method.report estimated reach
impression
A recorded presentation of content under the platform's definition.count impressions
eligible start
A video start included under the report's specified rules.define eligible starts
complete play
A playback reaching the specified end point.count complete plays
completion rate
Complete plays divided by the defined eligible-start base.calculate completion rate
view threshold
The condition a platform uses to count a view.check the view threshold
watch time
The total time spent viewing content under the specified method.analyze watch time
average view duration
Viewing time divided by the relevant view count.compare average view duration
retention curve
A display of audience continuation across a video's timeline.inspect the retention curve
drop-off
Loss of viewers at a stage or time in playback.locate the drop-off
frequency
Average exposures per reached audience member on the stated basis.monitor exposure frequency
unique viewer
A distinct viewer estimated or counted under the measurement method.estimate unique viewers
repeat play
An additional playback rather than necessarily a new viewer.distinguish repeat plays
engagement
A defined audience interaction such as a reaction, comment, or share.define engagement
engagement rate
Defined interactions divided by a specified audience or exposure base.specify the engagement-rate denominator
click-through rate (CTR)
Clicks divided by the defined impression base.calculate click-through rate
cost per thousand (CPM)
Cost for one thousand impressions on the stated basis.compare CPM
cost per view (CPV)
Cost divided by the relevant counted views.define cost per view
cost per completed view
Cost divided by completed views under the specified rules.calculate cost per completed view
brand lift
A measured change in a brand-related outcome under a study design.assess brand lift
attribution window
The time interval used to connect an outcome to an exposure or action.specify the attribution window
measurement methodology
The rules and process used to produce a metric.document measurement methodology
benchmark
A defined reference used to interpret performance.choose a relevant benchmark
business outcome
A commercial result beyond the media exposure itself.measure business outcomes

Estimated reach is a distinct-account measure here, while starts count eligible playback events. Do not divide completions by reach and label it this report's completion rate. Without spend or business outcomes, cost efficiency and commercial impact remain unestablished.

C Notice the language

Do not make the meeting choose one flattering number

A client may want a simple success story while an analyst sees mixed evidence. Lead with the relevant gains and the important limitation in the same sentence. This avoids both selective praise and an equally selective claim that everything failed.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with both results
Complete plays increased, while completion rate declined.
State the count gain
Completions rose from eight hundred to twelve hundred.
State the rate change
The rate fell from eighty to sixty percent.
Use the correct unit
That is a decline of twenty percentage points.
Show the denominator
The later period had two thousand eligible starts.
Keep reach separate
Estimated reach rose from nine hundred to fifteen hundred accounts.
Avoid a viewer claim
Twelve hundred complete plays does not necessarily mean twelve hundred different people.
Name the measurement rule
Here, a completion means playback reached the end.
Request a useful diagnostic
Let us inspect where viewers drop off.
Preserve objective differences
A reach goal and a completion-rate goal are not the same target.
Limit the commercial conclusion
These figures do not establish sales impact.
Name the missing cost
Spend is not included in this report.
Avoid a platform shortcut
Confirm the view threshold before comparing another service.
Make the summary balanced
We reached more accounts and recorded more completions, but fewer starts finished proportionally.
Ask for the right benchmark
Which agreed objective should the result be compared against?
Close with an evidence gap
Cost efficiency and business outcomes still need separate data.
6 language and precision notes
While
Connects simultaneous gains and a decline without hiding either.
Percentage points
Describes an absolute difference between percentage rates.
Estimated reach
Preserves the measurement qualification attached to the audience count.
Does not necessarily
Blocks a false equivalence between events and distinct people.
Here
Limits the completion definition to the stated report.
Still need
Makes the remaining evidence requirement explicit.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Complete plays rose 50%, but completion rate fell 20 percentage points.

Complete plays rose from 800 to 1,200, while the rate fell from 80% to 60%.

2. Which denominator produces the stated Period B completion rate?

Answer and explanation

C. 2,000 eligible starts

This report explicitly divides complete plays by eligible starts, not by reach.

3. Which claim requires additional evidence?

Answer and explanation

A. Cost per completed view improved.

Cost per completed view requires spend data, which the briefing does not supply.

4. Which diagnostic addresses retention?

Answer and explanation

C. Inspect the playback retention curve for drop-off points.

A retention curve shows how viewing continues across the timeline and can locate drop-off patterns.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

More completed plays, but a lower completion rate

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dana: Account director / Felix: Audience analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

    The later period had twice as many starts, so the larger completion count does not mean the same rate.
  4. Felix
    Exactly. The 2 moved from eighty percent to sixty percent. Two thousand starts produced twelve hundred completions, while the earlier thousand starts produced eight hundred.
  5. Dana
    That is twenty percentage points lower. The complete-play count itself is fifty percent higher.
  6. Felix
    Correct. Keep the 3 denominator visible. If we quietly switch to reached accounts, we are no longer calculating the completion rate defined in this report.
  7. Dana
    Reach rose from nine hundred to fifteen hundred accounts. Can I call those fifteen hundred viewers?
  8. Felix
    Preserve the report's 4 definition: estimated accounts. We should not strengthen an account estimate into a verified people count. That qualification matters when the client compares audience numbers.
  9. Dana
    And twelve hundred finished plays may include someone watching more than once.
  10. Felix
    Yes. A 5 can add another completion without adding a distinct account. Event counts and audience counts are useful, but they answer different questions.
  11. Dana
    What should we examine to understand the lower proportion finishing?
  12. Felix
    Inspect the 6. It can show where playback drops off. We should not invent the cause from the aggregate rate, but we can identify a useful place to investigate.

  14. Felix
