    I will report more reached accounts and more complete plays, alongside the lower completion rate and the missing cost and sales data.
  20. Felix
    Then compare those results with the agreed 10. A reach objective and a retention objective may receive different answers. We can be positive about the gains without claiming every objective was met.

Answers and explanations
  1. complete play Turn 2. Complete play is a playback reaching the end; its count rose from eight hundred to twelve hundred.
  2. completion rate Turn 4. Completion rate divides complete plays by eligible starts, giving eighty percent before and sixty percent afterward.
  3. eligible start Turn 6. Eligible start is the defined playback-event base for this rate; changing that base would change the measure.
  4. reach Turn 8. Reach is estimated distinct accounts here; it should not be silently relabeled as verified individual human viewers.
  5. repeat play Turn 10. Repeat play is another playback event and does not necessarily add a new distinct audience member.
  6. retention curve Turn 12. Retention curve shows continuation through the video timeline and can reveal where viewing falls away.
  7. measurement methodology Turn 14. Measurement methodology defines how the metrics were produced, supporting comparable interpretation across periods rather than assumed equivalence.
  8. cost per completed view Turn 16. Cost per completed view needs spend as well as completions; the provided data contains no spending figure.
  9. business outcome Turn 18. Business outcome concerns a commercial result beyond exposure; the audience figures alone do not establish sales impact.
  10. benchmark Turn 20. Benchmark is the defined reference or objective for judging performance; different goals may lead to different interpretations of the mixed results.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Wider reach, weaker completion

An audience analyst challenges the lesson's unqualified success claim: campaign reach increased while completion rate declined.

  1. Campaign manager: The report calls the video an unqualified success because it reached more viewers this time.
  2. Audience analyst: Reach increased, but completion rate declined. Both observations belong in the performance summary.
  3. Campaign manager: Does the lower completion rate prove that the creative became less engaging?
  4. Audience analyst: No. We haven't established the cause or checked whether the audience segment mix changed.
  5. Campaign manager: Then how can we explain the result without making the report sound indecisive?
  6. Audience analyst: State the wider reach and lower completion clearly, then identify what we still need to investigate.
  7. Campaign manager: Could retention by segment help us see where people stopped watching?
  8. Audience analyst: It could help describe viewing patterns, provided we use comparable definitions and time periods.
  9. Campaign manager: I'll remove the blanket success label and connect each measure to the campaign's intended objective.
  10. Audience analyst: I'll examine the segment data and keep explanations provisional until the evidence supports them.
The cliff at the opening titles

Separate fictional case: a streaming episode's retention chart drops at the opening-title sequence; no comparison with earlier episodes or device types has been made.

  1. Showrunner: The retention curve drops at the opening titles. Should we conclude viewers dislike the music?
  2. Audience researcher: The chart shows when viewing falls, but it doesn't tell us why people leave.
  3. Showrunner: So the timing is observed, while the explanation about music is still a hypothesis?
  4. Audience researcher: Exactly. We haven't compared other episodes or checked whether the pattern differs by device.
  5. Showrunner: Could a playback problem produce a similar drop without reflecting audience preference?
  6. Audience researcher: That's a possible alternative, not a confirmed issue. We can ask the platform team to check.
  7. Showrunner: I want a useful development note, not a claim that the opening has failed creatively.
  8. Audience researcher: Then say we observed a drop during the titles and are investigating audience and playback patterns.
  9. Showrunner: I'll keep any proposed edit separate from the finding and avoid blaming the composer.
  10. Audience researcher: I'll return with comparable retention evidence so we can discuss whether a test edit is warranted.
A higher percentage from a smaller group

Separate fictional case: two teaser tests use different invited audiences; A has 80 completions from 100 starts and B has 45 from 50 starts.

  1. Marketing producer: Teaser B has a ninety percent completion rate, so it must work better for everyone.
  2. Research analyst: B had forty-five completions from fifty starts. A had eighty from one hundred starts.
  3. Marketing producer: That is ninety percent versus eighty percent. Isn't that enough to choose the stronger creative?
  4. Research analyst: It describes these tests, but the invited audiences differed. We haven't isolated the creative's effect.
  5. Marketing producer: So B's higher rate doesn't establish that the same audience would prefer it?
  6. Research analyst: Correct. Also, A recorded more completed views in absolute numbers because its test group was larger.
  7. Marketing producer: Let's report both counts and rates instead of using whichever measure favors our preferred version.
  8. Research analyst: Agreed. We can propose a comparison with consistent audience selection before making a broad recommendation.
  9. Marketing producer: I'll describe B as having the higher observed completion rate in its test group.
  10. Research analyst: And I'll keep audience differences visible, rather than labeling that result a universal creative advantage.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

More completed plays, but a lower completion rate
  1. Read turns 1-10; report the completion count and rate together.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; name what cost and sales claims still require.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Explain another mixed audience result

Period C has 500 eligible starts and 350 complete plays. Period D has 800 eligible starts and 480 complete plays. Use completions divided by starts.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Director: "C's completion rate is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 70%

Three hundred fifty completions divided by five hundred starts equals seventy percent.

2. Analyst: "D's completion rate is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 60%

Four hundred eighty completions divided by eight hundred starts equals sixty percent.

3. Director: "The completion count rose by ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 130

Four hundred eighty minus three hundred fifty equals one hundred thirty additional complete plays.

4. Analyst: "The rate fell by ___."

Answer and explanation

C. ten percentage points

Seventy percent minus sixty percent is an absolute ten-percentage-point decline.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Audience Metrics and Performance
Original fictional case: Two comparable reporting periods cover the same 30-second video on one platform. Period A records 1,000 eligible starts, 800 complete plays, and estimated reach of 900 accounts. Period B records 2,000 eligible starts, 1,200 complete plays, and estimated reach of 1,500 accounts. A complete play reaches the end; completion rate is complete plays divided by eligible starts. Reach and starts are different measures. Spend and business-outcome data are not supplied. Analyst Felix and account director Dana must replace an unqualified success claim with a precise account of the gains and decline.
Language workshop: More completed plays, but a lower completion rate
Communication goal: Give a balanced performance readout using distinct counts, rates, and campaign objectives.
Language guidance: Complete plays increased by 400, or 50%, while completion rate fell from 80% to 60%, a decline of 20 percentage points. A larger audience can produce more completions even when a smaller share finishes.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Complete plays increased, while completion rate declined. / Completions rose from eight hundred to twelve hundred. / The rate fell from eighty to sixty percent. / That is a decline of twenty percentage points. / The later period had two thousand eligible starts. / Estimated reach rose from nine hundred to fifteen hundred accounts. / Twelve hundred complete plays does not necessarily mean twelve hundred different people. / Here, a completion means playback reached the end. / Let us inspect where viewers drop off. / A reach goal and a completion-rate goal are not the same target. / These figures do not establish sales impact. / Spend is not included in this report. / Confirm the view threshold before comparing another service. / We reached more accounts and recorded more completions, but fewer starts finished proportionally. / Which agreed objective should the result be compared against? / Cost efficiency and business outcomes still need separate data.
Vocabulary: reach: The estimated or measured distinct audience under a stated method. / impression: A recorded presentation of content under the platform's definition. / eligible start: A video start included under the report's specified rules. / complete play: A playback reaching the specified end point. / completion rate: Complete plays divided by the defined eligible-start base. / view threshold: The condition a platform uses to count a view. / watch time: The total time spent viewing content under the specified method. / average view duration: Viewing time divided by the relevant view count. / retention curve: A display of audience continuation across a video's timeline. / drop-off: Loss of viewers at a stage or time in playback. / frequency: Average exposures per reached audience member on the stated basis. / unique viewer: A distinct viewer estimated or counted under the measurement method. / repeat play: An additional playback rather than necessarily a new viewer. / engagement: A defined audience interaction such as a reaction, comment, or share. / engagement rate: Defined interactions divided by a specified audience or exposure base. / click-through rate (CTR): Clicks divided by the defined impression base. / cost per thousand (CPM): Cost for one thousand impressions on the stated basis. / cost per view (CPV): Cost divided by the relevant counted views. / cost per completed view: Cost divided by completed views under the specified rules. / brand lift: A measured change in a brand-related outcome under a study design. / attribution window: The time interval used to connect an outcome to an exposure or action. / measurement methodology: The rules and process used to produce a metric. / benchmark: A defined reference used to interpret performance. / business outcome: A commercial result beyond the media exposure itself.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; report the completion count and rate together. Swap roles for turns 11-20; name what cost and sales claims still require. Complete the transfer using eligible starts rather than reached accounts.
Partner: Dana: Account director / Felix: Audience analyst
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Period C has 500 eligible starts and 350 complete plays. Period D has 800 eligible starts and 480 complete plays. Use completions divided by starts.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dana: They may also want to know whether the campaign increased sales.
Felix: That business outcome needs separate evidence and an appropriate attribution approach. Neither the reach increase nor the completion change proves a sales effect.
Dana: I will report more reached accounts and more complete plays, alongside the lower completion rate and the missing cost and sales data.
Felix: Then compare those results with the agreed benchmark. A reach objective and a retention objective may receive different answers. We can be positive about the gains without claiming every objective was met.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Sponsorship, Brand Safety, and IntegrationOne extra mention changes more than the running time+

Your goal

Respond to a sponsor change by separating scope, factual claims, disclosure, and approval.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

One extra mention changes more than the running time

A sponsor has approved one ten-second product mention in a program. It now requests a second fifteen-second mention and proposes the line, "This product works for everyone." No evidence supporting that universal claim is supplied. The sponsor also asks to keep the sponsorship disclosure only in the video description. Account lead Carmen and producer Noel must review the added scope, editorial fit, truthful wording, and clear in-video disclosure for the endorsement. The extra mention, revised script, cost, and timing are not yet approved; the original mention alone does not approve the new request.

Carmen: Sponsorship account lead / Noel: Producer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is already approved?

Answer and explanation

D. One ten-second product mention

The briefing approves only the original ten-second mention, leaving the new request unresolved.

2. What is wrong with the proposed universal claim?

Answer and explanation

A. No supporting evidence is supplied.

The supplied facts do not support the assertion that the product works for everyone.

3. Where should the endorsement disclosure be addressed in this case?

Answer and explanation

D. Clearly in the video, not only in its description

The scenario requires clear in-video disclosure; a description-only approach can be missed by viewers.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The new request adds fifteen seconds of mention time, bringing proposed mention time to twenty-five seconds. That arithmetic does not establish the final program length, cost, editorial acceptability, or approval of the new wording.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
sponsorship
Commercial support linked to agreed exposure or association.disclose the sponsorship
brand integration
Incorporation of a brand or product into content.review the brand integration
product placement
Display or inclusion of a product within content.agree product placement
host-read
A promotional message delivered by the program's host.approve the host-read
endorsement
A message audiences are likely to understand as another party's opinion or experience.review the endorsement
material connection
A relationship that may affect how an audience evaluates an endorsement.disclose a material connection
disclosure
Information making a relevant commercial relationship clear.provide a clear disclosure
clear and conspicuous
Readily noticeable and understandable in the relevant context.make the disclosure clear and conspicuous
substantiation
Evidence supporting a factual claim.require claim substantiation
objective claim
A factual assertion capable of being assessed against evidence.verify an objective claim
universal claim
An assertion extending to every member of a stated group.challenge a universal claim
testimonial
A statement describing a person's experience or view.verify a testimonial
editorial fit
Compatibility with the content's purpose, tone, and audience.assess editorial fit
brand safety
Avoiding specified harmful or unsuitable brand associations.review brand-safety conditions
brand suitability
Compatibility with a particular brand's preferences and context.define brand suitability
integration plan
The agreed arrangement for incorporating sponsored material.revise the integration plan
sponsor deliverable
A specified item or exposure owed under the sponsorship agreement.confirm sponsor deliverables
category exclusivity
A restriction on competing sponsors within a defined category.check category exclusivity
makegood
Replacement value or exposure offered for a missed agreed delivery.negotiate a makegood
approval chain
The sequence of authorized content or commercial reviews.confirm the approval chain
script revision
A change to the wording of a production script.submit the script revision
scope change
A change to the work or output originally agreed.document the scope change
commercial break
A designated interval containing advertising.schedule the commercial break
audience trust
The audience's confidence in the content's honesty and reliability.protect audience trust

An endorsement disclosure must be clear in the actual viewing experience. A description-only disclosure may be missed. Disclosure also does not make an unsupported product claim true; claim review and relationship disclosure are separate tasks.

C Notice the language

Separate the client relationship from the content decision

An extra sponsor line can sound too small to challenge. Explain its concrete effects on time, editorial flow, claims, and approval. A constructive response offers a review path without allowing the commercial relationship to substitute for truthful, clearly identified content.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the request
The sponsor wants a second fifteen-second mention.
State the existing agreement
The current plan approves one ten-second mention.
Quantify the change
The proposed mentions would total twenty-five seconds.
Keep the scope visible
This is an additional deliverable, not an already approved adjustment.
Separate the reviews
Timing, cost, editorial fit, claims, and disclosure all need review.
Challenge the wording
What evidence supports the claim that it works for everyone?
Avoid a universal promise
We cannot present that line as established without support.
Protect truthful experience
Do not script a personal experience the host has not actually had.
Keep disclosure in the content
The endorsement needs a clear disclosure in the video itself.
Reject an inadequate shortcut
A description-only disclosure can be missed.
Preserve review status
The revised script has not been approved.
Check the impact
Which content would the extra fifteen seconds displace?
Confirm the decision-makers
Route the revision through the agreed approval chain.
Keep the price conditional
We need to price the added work before promising it.
Separate clarity and persuasion
A visible sponsorship disclosure does not excuse an unsupported claim.
Close with a bounded offer
I can submit a revised proposal, but I cannot confirm the new integration today.
6 language and precision notes
Would total
Calculates the proposed combined mention time without treating it as approved.
Already approved
Distinguishes existing scope from a new request.
What evidence supports
Challenges the factual basis rather than the sponsor's personality.
In the video itself
Locates disclosure where the endorsement is encountered.
Can be missed
Explains why an available disclosure may still be inadequate.
Has not actually had
Preserves the truth of a host's claimed personal experience.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement describes the time change accurately?

Answer and explanation

D. The proposed mentions total twenty-five seconds, fifteen more than approved.

Ten plus fifteen equals twenty-five seconds of mentions, not the entire program or an approved revision.

2. What does a clear disclosure not accomplish by itself?

Answer and explanation

D. It does not substantiate the universal product claim.

Disclosure addresses the relationship, while a factual product claim still needs its own supporting evidence.

3. Which response respects both the relationship and the review?

Answer and explanation

D. We can assess the extra mention, but the wording, disclosure, cost, and timing need approval.

The bounded response offers a path forward without inventing approval or accepting an unsupported claim.

4. Which disclosure approach fits the stated video endorsement?

Answer and explanation

D. A clear disclosure within the video, reviewed in context

Viewers need to encounter an understandable disclosure with the endorsement, not merely in internal or easily missed material.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

One extra mention changes more than the running time

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Carmen: Sponsorship account lead / Noel: Producer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • universal claim
  • audience trust
  • scope change
  • clear and conspicuous
  • testimonial
  • disclosure
  • editorial fit
  • Substantiation
  • integration plan
  • approval chain
  1. Carmen
    The sponsor wants another fifteen seconds after the agreed mention. They are calling it a small adjustment. Can we work out the actual impact before I answer?
  2. Noel
    Our 1 includes one ten-second mention. The request would bring mention time to twenty-five seconds, so we need to assess what changes in the edit and the agreement.
  3. Carmen
    They describe it as a small adjustment, but I can see it may displace something.
  4. Noel
    It is a 2. Let us review timing, cost, and editorial fit before promising it. The fact that the original mention is approved does not authorize the second one.
  5. Carmen
    Their suggested line is that the product works for everyone. They have not sent any evidence.
  6. Noel
    That 3 cannot be presented as established on this record. We need supporting evidence and an appropriate claims review, not simply a more confident delivery by the host.
  7. Carmen
    Could the host say it is what they personally experienced instead?
  8. Noel
    Only if the 4 is truthful. We should not manufacture personal experience to make an unsupported general statement sound acceptable. The wording needs to reflect what can genuinely be said.
  9. Carmen
    The sponsor also asked to keep the sponsorship note in the description so that it does not interrupt the program.
  10. Noel
    The 5 needs to work in the video itself. Viewers may never open the description. We should assess how they actually encounter the endorsement, not just whether a note exists somewhere.
  11. Carmen
    We can explain that a readable disclosure belongs in the viewing experience, not in an optional extra page.
  12. Noel
    Yes, it must be 6 in context. The final approach needs review with the actual video. A barely visible label or a platform setting should not be assumed sufficient on its own.
  13. Carmen
    If we fix the disclosure, the words works for everyone still need evidence, correct? I do not want the sponsor to think that one fix clears both issues.
  14. Noel
    Correct. 7 and disclosure address different questions. We need truthful content and a clear commercial relationship, not a choice between them.
  15. Carmen
    The extra segment may also feel repetitive after the host has already mentioned the product.
  16. Noel
    That is an 8 question. We can evaluate placement and repetition, but the commercial request should not silently overrule the program's purpose or the audience's experience.
  17. Carmen
    I will send the sponsor a proposal showing the added work, the revised wording route, and the disclosure requirement.
  18. Noel
    Route it through the 9. We still need the relevant decisions on the script, cost, timing, and integration. A proposal sent for review is not permission to publish.
  19. Carmen
    I can keep the response constructive: we will assess the request, but we cannot confirm the new segment today.
  20. Noel
    Exactly. That protects 10 and gives the sponsor a usable next step. We can work toward an effective integration without disguising paid content or making a claim the evidence does not support.

Answers and explanations
  1. integration plan Turn 2. Integration plan defines the original sponsored arrangement; it currently includes one ten-second mention, not the proposed additional segment.
  2. scope change Turn 4. Scope change adds work or output beyond the existing agreement; the extra mention cannot be treated as already approved.
  3. universal claim Turn 6. Universal claim extends the assertion to everyone, a breadth not supported by any evidence supplied in this scenario.
  4. testimonial Turn 8. Testimonial represents an actual person's experience or view; changing the grammar must not invent an experience the host did not have.
  5. disclosure Turn 10. Disclosure identifies the relevant commercial relationship; it needs to be clear in the video endorsement rather than solely in the description.
  6. clear and conspicuous Turn 12. Clear and conspicuous means readily noticeable and understandable in context, not hidden, fleeting, or dependent on opening a description.
  7. Substantiation Turn 14. Substantiation is the evidence supporting a factual claim; disclosing sponsorship does not supply that evidence.
  8. editorial fit Turn 16. Editorial fit assesses how the extra mention works with the program's purpose, tone, and audience rather than only its paid duration.
  9. approval chain Turn 18. Approval chain identifies the authorized reviews needed for the new script and commercial arrangement before commitment.
  10. audience trust Turn 20. Audience trust depends on truthful, identifiable commercial content; careful review supports both the production and the sponsor relationship.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Carmen: The sponsor wants another fifteen seconds after the agreed mention. They are calling it a small adjustment. Can we work out the actual impact before I answer?
  2. Noel: Our integration plan includes one ten-second mention. The request would bring mention time to twenty-five seconds, so we need to assess what changes in the edit and the agreement.
  3. Carmen: They describe it as a small adjustment, but I can see it may displace something.
  4. Noel: It is a scope change. Let us review timing, cost, and editorial fit before promising it. The fact that the original mention is approved does not authorize the second one.
  5. Carmen: Their suggested line is that the product works for everyone. They have not sent any evidence.
  6. Noel: That universal claim cannot be presented as established on this record. We need supporting evidence and an appropriate claims review, not simply a more confident delivery by the host.
  7. Carmen: Could the host say it is what they personally experienced instead?
  8. Noel: Only if the testimonial is truthful. We should not manufacture personal experience to make an unsupported general statement sound acceptable. The wording needs to reflect what can genuinely be said.
  9. Carmen: The sponsor also asked to keep the sponsorship note in the description so that it does not interrupt the program.
  10. Noel: The disclosure needs to work in the video itself. Viewers may never open the description. We should assess how they actually encounter the endorsement, not just whether a note exists somewhere.
  11. Carmen: We can explain that a readable disclosure belongs in the viewing experience, not in an optional extra page.
  12. Noel: Yes, it must be clear and conspicuous in context. The final approach needs review with the actual video. A barely visible label or a platform setting should not be assumed sufficient on its own.
  13. Carmen: If we fix the disclosure, the words works for everyone still need evidence, correct? I do not want the sponsor to think that one fix clears both issues.
  14. Noel: Correct. Substantiation and disclosure address different questions. We need truthful content and a clear commercial relationship, not a choice between them.
  15. Carmen: The extra segment may also feel repetitive after the host has already mentioned the product.
  16. Noel: That is an editorial fit question. We can evaluate placement and repetition, but the commercial request should not silently overrule the program's purpose or the audience's experience.
  17. Carmen: I will send the sponsor a proposal showing the added work, the revised wording route, and the disclosure requirement.
  18. Noel: Route it through the approval chain. We still need the relevant decisions on the script, cost, timing, and integration. A proposal sent for review is not permission to publish.
  19. Carmen: I can keep the response constructive: we will assess the request, but we cannot confirm the new segment today.
  20. Noel: Exactly. That protects audience trust and gives the sponsor a usable next step. We can work toward an effective integration without disguising paid content or making a claim the evidence does not support.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An extra mention changes the integration

The lesson's sponsor requests another product mention after the integration plan has been agreed; no extra fee or schedule flexibility is established.

  1. Sponsor manager: Could you add another product mention? We would like more visibility than the agreed integration provides.
  2. Branded-content producer: We can discuss it, but an additional mention changes the deliverable we planned together.
  3. Sponsor manager: Would repeating the existing line avoid any meaningful change to the production work?
  4. Branded-content producer: Not necessarily. We need to assess pacing, recording requirements, and brand safety before confirming that.
  5. Sponsor manager: What alternatives could you explore without promising a new version immediately?
  6. Branded-content producer: We could review replacing an existing mention or assess the work needed for an additional one.
  7. Sponsor manager: Please compare those options. Our preference is more visibility, but we haven't approved extra expenditure.
  8. Branded-content producer: Understood. Any added scope would need agreement on cost and timing before production proceeds.
  9. Sponsor manager: And let's not call this a makegood; we haven't established a missed contractual deliverable.
  10. Branded-content producer: Agreed. I'll bring a scoped change proposal and keep the current integration plan in place meanwhile.
A sponsor category conflicts with a segment

Separate fictional case: a fictional podcast sponsor sells sleep products, while a planned comedy segment mocks sleep-tracking gadgets; the approved integration has not yet been recorded.

  1. Partnership producer: The planned gadget segment may raise a brand safety concern for our sleep-product sponsor.
  2. Editorial producer: I can review the transition, but I don't want sponsorship to silently dictate the editorial discussion.
  3. Partnership producer: Could we separate the product placement from that segment instead of changing the editorial point?
  4. Editorial producer: Possibly. We need to compare that with the agreed placement and preserve a clear sponsor disclosure.
  5. Partnership producer: Would you consider moving the integration to another break, subject to the sponsor's agreement?
  6. Editorial producer: Yes, provided the timing works and nobody presents it as already approved by either side.
  7. Partnership producer: I'll ask whether the alternate break meets their needs without promising an additional mention.
  8. Editorial producer: And I'll check the episode structure. We may need another discussion if the move disrupts pacing.
  9. Partnership producer: Then the proposal is a placement change, not extra editorial control or an expanded deliverable.
  10. Editorial producer: Correct. We'll confirm the revised arrangement only after both production and partnership checks are complete.
Choose a makegood that can actually run

Separate fictional case: a sponsor's contracted newsletter mention was omitted. The publisher proposes either a mention in next week's edition or one in a themed edition next month; neither is yet accepted.

  1. Account manager: We missed the newsletter mention and need to agree a makegood that addresses the omitted deliverable.
  2. Sponsor representative: Could you place it in both editions to compensate for the disruption to our campaign?
  3. Account manager: I can bring that request for approval, but the current options cover one replacement mention.
  4. Sponsor representative: Next week is sooner, whereas the themed edition next month fits our product more closely.
  5. Account manager: Exactly. We can discuss timing and context without guaranteeing a particular audience response.
  6. Sponsor representative: If we choose next month, can you confirm the precise placement before we accept?
  7. Account manager: I'll check available placement and return with the proposed deliverable. It isn't reserved yet.
  8. Sponsor representative: Please include the publication date and copy-review arrangements so the replacement is unambiguous.
  9. Account manager: Agreed. I'll seek those confirmations and your acceptance before calling the makegood settled.
  10. Sponsor representative: That works. We'll weigh the later but more relevant context against the earlier campaign exposure.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

One extra mention changes more than the running time
  1. Read turns 1-10; separate the new scope request from the existing approval.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish clear disclosure from claim substantiation.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify another integration request

An approved plan contains one eight-second mention. A sponsor requests another twelve-second mention. The additional work and a new factual claim are unapproved; no supporting evidence is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Account lead: "The approved mention is ___ seconds."

Answer and explanation

B. eight

Eight seconds is the existing approved mention, not the requested addition.

2. Producer: "The requested addition is ___ seconds."

Answer and explanation

A. twelve

Twelve seconds is the duration of the new, still-unapproved request.

3. Account lead: "The proposed mention time would total ___ seconds."

Answer and explanation

D. twenty

Eight approved seconds plus twelve requested seconds totals twenty proposed seconds.

4. Producer: "The new factual claim still needs ___."

Answer and explanation

D. substantiation

The briefing supplies no supporting evidence, so the claim requires substantiation rather than confident repetition.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Sponsorship, Brand Safety, and Integration
Original fictional case: A sponsor has approved one ten-second product mention in a program. It now requests a second fifteen-second mention and proposes the line, "This product works for everyone." No evidence supporting that universal claim is supplied. The sponsor also asks to keep the sponsorship disclosure only in the video description. Account lead Carmen and producer Noel must review the added scope, editorial fit, truthful wording, and clear in-video disclosure for the endorsement. The extra mention, revised script, cost, and timing are not yet approved; the original mention alone does not approve the new request.
Language workshop: One extra mention changes more than the running time
Communication goal: Respond to a sponsor change by separating scope, factual claims, disclosure, and approval.
Language guidance: The new request adds fifteen seconds of mention time, bringing proposed mention time to twenty-five seconds. That arithmetic does not establish the final program length, cost, editorial acceptability, or approval of the new wording.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The sponsor wants a second fifteen-second mention. / The current plan approves one ten-second mention. / The proposed mentions would total twenty-five seconds. / This is an additional deliverable, not an already approved adjustment. / Timing, cost, editorial fit, claims, and disclosure all need review. / What evidence supports the claim that it works for everyone? / We cannot present that line as established without support. / Do not script a personal experience the host has not actually had. / The endorsement needs a clear disclosure in the video itself. / A description-only disclosure can be missed. / The revised script has not been approved. / Which content would the extra fifteen seconds displace? / Route the revision through the agreed approval chain. / We need to price the added work before promising it. / A visible sponsorship disclosure does not excuse an unsupported claim. / I can submit a revised proposal, but I cannot confirm the new integration today.
Vocabulary: sponsorship: Commercial support linked to agreed exposure or association. / brand integration: Incorporation of a brand or product into content. / product placement: Display or inclusion of a product within content. / host-read: A promotional message delivered by the program's host. / endorsement: A message audiences are likely to understand as another party's opinion or experience. / material connection: A relationship that may affect how an audience evaluates an endorsement. / disclosure: Information making a relevant commercial relationship clear. / clear and conspicuous: Readily noticeable and understandable in the relevant context. / substantiation: Evidence supporting a factual claim. / objective claim: A factual assertion capable of being assessed against evidence. / universal claim: An assertion extending to every member of a stated group. / testimonial: A statement describing a person's experience or view. / editorial fit: Compatibility with the content's purpose, tone, and audience. / brand safety: Avoiding specified harmful or unsuitable brand associations. / brand suitability: Compatibility with a particular brand's preferences and context. / integration plan: The agreed arrangement for incorporating sponsored material. / sponsor deliverable: A specified item or exposure owed under the sponsorship agreement. / category exclusivity: A restriction on competing sponsors within a defined category. / makegood: Replacement value or exposure offered for a missed agreed delivery. / approval chain: The sequence of authorized content or commercial reviews. / script revision: A change to the wording of a production script. / scope change: A change to the work or output originally agreed. / commercial break: A designated interval containing advertising. / audience trust: The audience's confidence in the content's honesty and reliability.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; separate the new scope request from the existing approval. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish clear disclosure from claim substantiation. Complete the transfer and retain the difference between approved and proposed duration.
Partner: Carmen: Sponsorship account lead / Noel: Producer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An approved plan contains one eight-second mention. A sponsor requests another twelve-second mention. The additional work and a new factual claim are unapproved; no supporting evidence is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Carmen: I will send the sponsor a proposal showing the added work, the revised wording route, and the disclosure requirement.
Noel: Route it through the approval chain. We still need the relevant decisions on the script, cost, timing, and integration. A proposal sent for review is not permission to publish.
Carmen: I can keep the response constructive: we will assess the request, but we cannot confirm the new segment today.
Noel: Exactly. That protects audience trust and gives the sponsor a usable next step. We can work toward an effective integration without disguising paid content or making a claim the evidence does not support.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Vocabulary: sponsorship: Commercial support linked to agreed exposure or association. / brand integration: Incorporation of a brand or product into content. / product placement: Display or inclusion of a product within content. / host-read: A promotional message delivered by the program's host. / endorsement: A message audiences are likely to understand as another party's opinion or experience. / material connection: A relationship that may affect how an audience evaluates an endorsement. / disclosure: Information making a relevant commercial relationship clear. / clear and conspicuous: Readily noticeable and understandable in the relevant context. / substantiation: Evidence supporting a factual claim. / objective claim: A factual assertion capable of being assessed against evidence. / universal claim: An assertion extending to every member of a stated group. / testimonial: A statement describing a person's experience or view. / editorial fit: Compatibility with the content's purpose, tone, and audience. / brand safety: Avoiding specified harmful or unsuitable brand associations. / brand suitability: Compatibility with a particular brand's preferences and context. / integration plan: The agreed arrangement for incorporating sponsored material. / sponsor deliverable: A specified item or exposure owed under the sponsorship agreement. / category exclusivity: A restriction on competing sponsors within a defined category. / makegood: Replacement value or exposure offered for a missed agreed delivery. / approval chain: The sequence of authorized content or commercial reviews. / script revision: A change to the wording of a production script. / scope change: A change to the work or output originally agreed. / commercial break: A designated interval containing advertising. / audience trust: The audience's confidence in the content's honesty and reliability.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; separate the new scope request from the existing approval. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish clear disclosure from claim substantiation. Complete the transfer and retain the difference between approved and proposed duration.
Partner: Carmen: Sponsorship account lead / Noel: Producer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An approved plan contains one eight-second mention. A sponsor requests another twelve-second mention. The additional work and a new factual claim are unapproved; no supporting evidence is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Carmen: I will send the sponsor a proposal showing the added work, the revised wording route, and the disclosure requirement.
Noel: Route it through the approval chain. We still need the relevant decisions on the script, cost, timing, and integration. A proposal sent for review is not permission to publish.
Carmen: I can keep the response constructive: we will assess the request, but we cannot confirm the new segment today.
Noel: Exactly. That protects audience trust and gives the sponsor a usable next step. We can work toward an effective integration without disguising paid content or making a claim the evidence does not support.
END REFERENCE
08Crisis Response and Public StatementsConfirm the production pause, not an unverified cause+

Your goal

Prepare a concise holding statement that separates verified facts, open questions, and the next update.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Confirm the production pause, not an unverified cause

Filming at Studio C paused at 09:20 following a technical interruption. That pause and time are verified. The cause remains under investigation, injury reports are unverified, and no restart time is confirmed. A draft statement blames a contractor and says nobody was hurt; neither claim is supported. Communications manager Farah and executive producer Luis must remove those claims. They can state the verified pause, say the situation is being assessed, and commit to an update at 11:00. Only the designated spokesperson may release the approved statement under the local process.

Farah: Communications manager / Luis: Executive producer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which fact is verified?

Answer and explanation

A. Filming at Studio C paused at 09:20.

The brief verifies the pause, location, and time while leaving cause, injury status, and restart unresolved.

2. Which claims must be removed from the draft?

Answer and explanation

A. Contractor blame and the unsupported no-injury claim

Neither contractor responsibility nor absence of injury is supported by the supplied facts.

3. What does 11:00 represent?

Answer and explanation

D. The next public update

Eleven o'clock is the communication commitment, not a guarantee of operational or investigative completion.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Not confirmed is not the same as false, and unverified injury reports do not establish either injury or no injury. State the verification boundary instead of choosing the more reassuring conclusion.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
holding statement
A brief initial statement using verified information while further facts are checked.prepare a holding statement
verified fact
Information confirmed through an adequate evidential basis.separate verified facts
unverified report
Information received but not yet confirmed.label an unverified report
source attribution
Identifying where information came from.preserve source attribution
corroboration
Supporting confirmation from additional relevant evidence.seek corroboration
incident timeline
A chronological record of relevant events.establish the incident timeline
designated spokesperson
The person authorized to speak publicly for the organization.brief the designated spokesperson
media inquiry
A request for information from a journalist or outlet.log a media inquiry
public statement
An authorized communication intended for a public audience.approve the public statement
on the record
Information provided for attributable publication under agreed ground rules.clarify on-the-record terms
off the record
A source-use arrangement requiring explicit mutual understanding.agree off-the-record terms
embargo
An agreed restriction on publication until a stated time or condition.confirm the embargo
correction
A visible acknowledgment and repair of an error.issue a correction
clarification
Additional explanation making an earlier statement clearer.provide a clarification
speculation
An explanation or claim not established by the available evidence.avoid speculation
causal claim
A statement asserting why an event occurred.verify a causal claim
welfare update
Information about affected people's condition or support, within verified and authorized limits.verify a welfare update
privacy boundary
A limit on sharing personal information.respect privacy boundaries
operational pause
A temporary stop in activity.confirm the operational pause
restart authorization
Permission to resume activity under the relevant process.verify restart authorization
stakeholder briefing
An update to people with a relevant interest or responsibility.coordinate a stakeholder briefing
message consistency
Agreement among communications on the verified facts and status.maintain message consistency
approval version
The specific statement text cleared for release.identify the approval version
update cadence
The planned rhythm or schedule of further communications.establish an update cadence

An 11:00 update is not an 11:00 restart. If later evidence changes a published fact, correct it visibly through the authorized process. Calling a factual correction a clarification must not hide the original error.

C Notice the language

Precision is not indifference

A careful holding statement can acknowledge concern without asserting a cause, injury status, or restart time that has not been verified. Avoid defensive certainty. Clear limits and a reliable next update are more credible than a complete-sounding explanation assembled from rumor.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the verified event
Filming at Studio C paused at 09:20.
Describe the known context
The pause followed a technical interruption.
Limit the explanation
The cause has not yet been confirmed.
Remove unsupported blame
We do not have a verified basis for naming a responsible party.
Keep welfare information accurate
Injury reports have not yet been verified.
Avoid an unsupported reassurance
We cannot state that nobody was hurt on this record.
State the ongoing action
The situation is being assessed.
Commit to the next contact
We will provide an update at 11:00.
Separate time promises
That is an update time, not a confirmed restart.
Protect personal information
Release only verified and authorized personal details.
Name the release route
The designated spokesperson will issue the approved statement.
Preserve the evidence
Keep source and timestamp information with each factual claim.
Align internal messages
Brief the team on the same verified facts and open questions.
Handle a media question
I cannot confirm that explanation; we will update the verified position.
Correct an actual error
If published information is wrong, acknowledge and correct it visibly.
Close the draft review
Remove the unsupported claims and retain the factual pause and update commitment.
6 language and precision notes
Followed
Gives sequence or context without necessarily proving the underlying cause.
Not yet confirmed
States an evidential limit rather than denying the event or explanation.
On this record
Limits the conclusion to the available support.
Will provide an update
Commits to communication rather than a guaranteed resolution.
Verified and authorized
Requires both factual support and permission for disclosure.
Acknowledge and correct
Prevents a silent rewrite from concealing a published factual error.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which holding statement fits the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. Filming at Studio C paused at 09:20 following a technical interruption. We are assessing the situation and will update at 11:00.

The supported statement uses the verified pause and timing while avoiding unsupported cause, injury, or restart claims.

2. What does unverified injury information justify saying?

Answer and explanation

D. Injury reports have not yet been verified.

Lack of verification supports only the stated uncertainty, not either a positive or negative injury finding.

3. Who may release the statement under the local process?

Answer and explanation

C. The designated spokesperson, using the approved version

The briefing assigns public release to the designated spokesperson and the approved text.

4. If a published factual claim proves wrong, what is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

D. Acknowledge and correct the error visibly through the authorized process.

Accurate correction must repair the record transparently rather than hide a substantive published error.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Confirm the production pause, not an unverified cause

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Farah: Communications manager / Luis: Executive producer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • restart authorization
  • correction
  • welfare update
  • holding statement
  • causal claim
  • verified fact
  • designated spokesperson
  • unverified report
  • approval version
  • incident timeline
  1. Luis
    Who supplied the contractor-error and no-injury lines? They are in the draft, but I cannot trace either one to a confirmed source.
  2. Farah
    Then neither belongs in the 1. We have confirmed that filming at Studio C paused at nine twenty following a technical interruption. The cause and injury reports are still being checked.
  3. Luis
    We should acknowledge concern without presenting the draft's explanation as fact.
  4. Farah
    Exactly. Separate each 2 from the open questions. We can say the situation is being assessed and that we will provide an update at eleven.
  5. Luis
    Someone on the crew says the contractor was working near the equipment. Does that justify naming them?
  6. Farah
    That is not enough for a 3. Being nearby does not establish responsibility. We need an adequate verified basis before asserting a cause or naming a party as responsible.
  7. Luis
    I have also seen social posts about injuries. We cannot confirm them yet.
  8. Farah
    Treat each as an 4. That does not mean the report is false, and it does not justify saying nobody was hurt. We must preserve the actual verification status.
  9. Luis
    We can ask the appropriate people for confirmed information without releasing private details in the meantime.
  10. Farah
    Yes. A 5 needs both accuracy and the proper disclosure basis. Concern for people should not become a reason to circulate names or conditions we are not authorized to share.
  11. Luis
    Please make the eleven-o'clock line explicitly an update. I do not want crews or reporters reading it as a restart time.
  12. Farah
    Then distinguish it from 6. Eleven is the next communication time. We do not have a confirmed restart time, and the statement must not imply one.
  13. Luis
    Who should send the public version? Several department leads are receiving media questions.
  14. Farah
    The 7 will issue the approved text. We should brief the department leads on the same facts and route inquiries through the agreed process rather than improvise separate explanations.
  15. Luis
    Let us also keep track of when each fact was confirmed and by whom.
  16. Farah
    Maintain the 8 with source information. That will help us update accurately as evidence develops and avoid confusing the time of a report with the time of the event.
  17. Luis
    If something we publish later proves wrong, we should not just quietly replace the text.
  18. Farah
    Correct. Issue a visible 9 through the authorized process. A clarification can explain wording, but it should not be used to disguise a substantive error in what we said.
  19. Luis
    I will remove the blame and no-injury sentence. The remaining statement will give the pause, ongoing assessment, and eleven-o'clock update.
  20. Farah
    I will identify that 10 for review and release. The public should receive the verified position, not the most reassuring draft. We can be clear, humane, and precise while questions remain open.

Answers and explanations
  1. holding statement Turn 2. Holding statement provides a brief verified update while open questions are checked; it must not invent a complete explanation.
  2. verified fact Turn 4. Verified fact has adequate confirmation; here the pause, location, and time are established while other claims remain unresolved.
  3. causal claim Turn 6. Causal claim explains why an event occurred; proximity to equipment does not establish responsibility for the interruption.
  4. unverified report Turn 8. Unverified report is information not yet confirmed; it does not establish either an injury finding or the absence of injuries.
  5. welfare update Turn 10. Welfare update concerns affected people and must use verified information within the relevant authorization and privacy limits.
  6. restart authorization Turn 12. Restart authorization permits resuming activity; the scheduled public update does not supply that permission or a restart time.
  7. designated spokesperson Turn 14. Designated spokesperson is the person authorized under this local process to release the approved public statement.
  8. incident timeline Turn 16. Incident timeline orders relevant events and their support, helping the team distinguish verified times from rumors and later interpretations.
  9. correction Turn 18. Correction acknowledges and repairs a factual error; a silent replacement would conceal the earlier incorrect public claim.
  10. approval version Turn 20. Approval version is the specific text cleared for release, preventing an obsolete or unsupported draft from becoming the public statement.

Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Remove the unverified explanation

A communications lead reviews the lesson's draft public statement about a production incident while facts are still being verified.

  1. Communications lead: The draft public statement explains why the production incident happened. Has that explanation been verified?
  2. Production spokesperson: Not yet. The team is still checking the facts, so that sentence goes beyond the evidence.
  3. Communications lead: Could we use a holding statement that acknowledges the incident without assigning a cause?
  4. Production spokesperson: Yes. We should separate what has been confirmed from what remains under review.
  5. Communications lead: The team worries that acknowledging uncertainty will make the statement seem evasive during the backlash.
  6. Production spokesperson: An unsupported explanation would create another problem. We can be direct about the limits of our information.
  7. Communications lead: Should we promise a full account tomorrow to make the holding statement more reassuring?
  8. Production spokesperson: Only if that timing is established. We don't currently have a verified completion date for the review.
  9. Communications lead: I'll remove the causal claim and submit the factual wording through the communications and legal review process.
  10. Production spokesperson: I'll check each operational claim and keep the next update tied to verified information.
A circulating clip is not the full recording

Separate fictional case: a shortened rehearsal clip is circulating online; the production team has the full recording but has not yet compared the two versions.

  1. Press officer: People are criticizing the rehearsal clip. Can we say it was deliberately edited to mislead viewers?
  2. Production researcher: We haven't compared it with the full recording yet, so we can't establish that claim.
  3. Press officer: We do know the circulating clip is shorter. Isn't that enough to call it misleading?
  4. Production researcher: It establishes that material is omitted, not the editor's intention or the effect of the omissions.
  5. Press officer: Then our holding statement should avoid attributing motive to the person who shared it.
  6. Production researcher: Yes. We can say we're reviewing the footage, if that review has actually begun.
  7. Press officer: I'll ask the authorized team to start the comparison before describing it as underway.
  8. Production researcher: Please also keep individual names out of the draft unless their inclusion has been reviewed.
  9. Press officer: I'll prepare restrained wording and seek approval, without promising that the full recording exonerates anyone.
  10. Production researcher: I'll provide a comparison of the actual content, with any uncertainty about context clearly noted.
A cancelled screening and an unknown cause

Separate fictional case: a screening was cancelled after a projector stopped working; the venue has not established whether the fault was equipment, power, or another cause.

  1. Festival communications manager: Our draft says the venue failed to maintain its projector. Do we have evidence for that?
  2. Venue operations lead: No. We know the projector stopped working and the screening was cancelled; the cause remains unknown.
  3. Festival communications manager: I will remove the maintenance allegation. Can we still acknowledge the inconvenience to ticket holders?
  4. Venue operations lead: Certainly. Acknowledging their disrupted evening doesn't require us to speculate about the technical fault.
  5. Festival communications manager: People are asking about another screening. Is a replacement date already confirmed?
  6. Venue operations lead: Not yet. We should distinguish exploring another screening from announcing a scheduled replacement.
  7. Festival communications manager: Then the public statement can state the cancellation and say scheduling information is not yet confirmed.
  8. Venue operations lead: Yes. Route any claims about remedies through the responsible ticketing team before publishing them.
  9. Festival communications manager: I'll use verified facts and avoid assigning blame while we obtain the operational and ticketing updates.
  10. Venue operations lead: I'll provide the confirmed information as it becomes available, with the unresolved cause kept explicit.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

Course: Media and Entertainment English
Professional audience: producers, production managers, creative executives, rights coordinators, distribution teams, talent managers, marketing staff, and media operations professionals
Scope: All productions, clients, performers, incidents, figures, and contract terms are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not legal advice or production-safety procedures. Rights, labor conditions, disclosures, and approvals depend on the actual agreements, uses, jurisdiction, and current requirements. Lesson 08: Crisis Response and Public Statements
Original fictional case: Filming at Studio C paused at 09:20 following a technical interruption. That pause and time are verified. The cause remains under investigation, injury reports are unverified, and no restart time is confirmed. A draft statement blames a contractor and says nobody was hurt; neither claim is supported. Communications manager Farah and executive producer Luis must remove those claims. They can state the verified pause, say the situation is being assessed, and commit to an update at 11:00. Only the designated spokesperson may release the approved statement under the local process.
Language workshop: Confirm the production pause, not an unverified cause
Communication goal: Prepare a concise holding statement that separates verified facts, open questions, and the next update.
Language guidance: Not confirmed is not the same as false, and unverified injury reports do not establish either injury or no injury. State the verification boundary instead of choosing the more reassuring conclusion.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Filming at Studio C paused at 09:20. / The pause followed a technical interruption. / The cause has not yet been confirmed. / We do not have a verified basis for naming a responsible party. / Injury reports have not yet been verified. / We cannot state that nobody was hurt on this record. / The situation is being assessed. / We will provide an update at 11:00. / That is an update time, not a confirmed restart. / Release only verified and authorized personal details. / The designated spokesperson will issue the approved statement. / Keep source and timestamp information with each factual claim. / Brief the team on the same verified facts and open questions. / I cannot confirm that explanation; we will update the verified position. / If published information is wrong, acknowledge and correct it visibly. / Remove the unsupported claims and retain the factual pause and update commitment.
Vocabulary: holding statement: A brief initial statement using verified information while further facts are checked. / verified fact: Information confirmed through an adequate evidential basis. / unverified report: Information received but not yet confirmed. / source attribution: Identifying where information came from. / corroboration: Supporting confirmation from additional relevant evidence. / incident timeline: A chronological record of relevant events. / designated spokesperson: The person authorized to speak publicly for the organization. / media inquiry: A request for information from a journalist or outlet. / public statement: An authorized communication intended for a public audience. / on the record: Information provided for attributable publication under agreed ground rules. / off the record: A source-use arrangement requiring explicit mutual understanding. / embargo: An agreed restriction on publication until a stated time or condition. / correction: A visible acknowledgment and repair of an error. / clarification: Additional explanation making an earlier statement clearer. / speculation: An explanation or claim not established by the available evidence. / causal claim: A statement asserting why an event occurred. / welfare update: Information about affected people's condition or support, within verified and authorized limits. / privacy boundary: A limit on sharing personal information. / operational pause: A temporary stop in activity. / restart authorization: Permission to resume activity under the relevant process. / stakeholder briefing: An update to people with a relevant interest or responsibility. / message consistency: Agreement among communications on the verified facts and status. / approval version: The specific statement text cleared for release. / update cadence: The planned rhythm or schedule of further communications.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; keep verified events separate from cause and welfare reports. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the update appointment from permission to restart. Complete the transfer and read back the event time, update time, and spokesperson.
Partner: Farah: Communications manager / Luis: Executive producer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Recording at Studio D paused at 15:10. The cause and restart time are unconfirmed. The next approved status update is 16:00, to be delivered by spokesperson Rina.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Luis: If something we publish later proves wrong, we should not just quietly replace the text.
Farah: Correct. Issue a visible correction through the authorized process. A clarification can explain wording, but it should not be used to disguise a substantive error in what we said.
Luis: I will remove the blame and no-injury sentence. The remaining statement will give the pause, ongoing assessment, and eleven-o'clock update.
Farah: I will identify that approval version for review and release. The public should receive the verified position, not the most reassuring draft. We can be clear, humane, and precise while questions remain open.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.
